# 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles of operation, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, electrochemistry, mass spectrometry, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments can be used for various purposes, such as quality control, research and development, environmental monitoring, forensic analysis, clinical diagnosis, etc.
- Analytical instruments can provide qualitative or quantitative information about the samples, such as their identity, composition, structure, concentration, purity, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different levels of complexity, sensitivity, accuracy, precision, and selectivity, depending on their design, performance, and application.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as sources, detectors, transducers, signal processors, data analyzers, etc., that work together to produce and interpret the analytical signals.
- Analytical instruments can have different modes of operation, such as continuous, discrete, batch, or flow, depending on how the samples are introduced and processed.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as speed, cost, reliability, robustness, portability, etc., depending on their features and specifications.



# Analysis of Chloride Content, Hardness, and Alkalinity

- Chloride content is a measure of the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in a water sample. Chloride ions are one of the major dissolved constituents of natural waters and can originate from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, or road salt.
- Hardness is a measure of the concentration of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions in a water sample. These ions can cause scaling, corrosion, and soap precipitation in water systems and appliances. Hardness can be expressed as total hardness, carbonate hardness, or non-carbonate hardness, depending on the presence of bicarbonate (HCO3-) and carbonate (CO32-) ions in the water.
- Alkalinity is a measure of the capacity of a water sample to neutralize acids. It is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide ions in the water, but other bases, such as ammonia, borate, phosphate, and silicate, can also contribute. Alkalinity is important for maintaining the pH and buffering capacity of water.

## Methods of Analysis

- Chloride content can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of silver nitrate (AgNO3) using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4).
- Hardness can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) using eriochrome black T (EBT) as an indicator. The end point of the titration is marked by a color change from wine red to blue. The total hardness can be obtained by titrating the water sample without any prior treatment. The carbonate hardness can be obtained by boiling the water sample to expel carbon dioxide (CO2) and then titrating the cooled sample. The non-carbonate hardness can be obtained by subtracting the carbonate hardness from the total hardness.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators. The phenolphthalein alkalinity is obtained by titrating the water sample until the pink color disappears. The methyl orange alkalinity is obtained by continuing the titration until the yellow color turns orange. The total alkalinity can be calculated from the volumes of acid used for each titration.



# Water

- Water is a substance composed of the chemical elements hydrogen and oxygen and existing in gaseous, liquid, and solid states.
- Water is a tiny molecule that consists of three atoms: two of hydrogen and one of oxygen. Water molecules cling to each other because of a force called hydrogen bonding.
- Water is one of the most plentiful and essential of compounds. It has the important ability to dissolve many other substances. It is also a shape-shifter that exists in three states on Earth: liquid, gas, and solid .
- Water is the liquid that makes life on Earth possible. All living things, from tiny cyanobacteria to giant blue whales, need water to survive. Water cycles from the air to the land to the sea and back again, water shapes our planet and nearly every aspect of our lives.
- Water is also a vital resource for human activities, such as drinking, agriculture, industry, and sanitation. Water supply and quality are affected by natural factors, such as climate, geology, and ecology, as well as human factors, such as population, consumption, and pollution .
- Water is an inorganic compound with the chemical formula H2O. It is a transparent, tasteless, odorless, and nearly colorless chemical substance, and it is the main constituent of Earth's hydrosphere and the fluids of all known living organisms (in which it acts as a solvent).



# 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a solution is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. pH = -log[H+]
- The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, with 7 being neutral, lower than 7 being acidic, and higher than 7 being basic. The pH of pure water is 7 at 25°C.
- pH can be measured using indicators, such as litmus paper or phenolphthalein, which change color depending on the acidity or basicity of the solution. Alternatively, pH can be measured using a pH meter, which is an electronic device that measures the voltage difference between a reference electrode and a pH-sensitive electrode immersed in the solution.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is defined as the force per unit length required to stretch or break the surface of a liquid. Surface tension = F/L
- Surface tension causes liquids to form spherical droplets, to minimize their surface area. It also allows some insects, such as water striders, to walk on the surface of water without sinking.
- Surface tension can be measured using a force balance, which measures the force required to pull a ring or a plate out of the liquid surface. Alternatively, surface tension can be measured using a drop weight method, which measures the weight of a droplet of liquid that detaches from a needle or a capillary tube.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a fluid is to flow. It is defined as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in a fluid. Viscosity = τ/γ
- The shear stress is the force per unit area exerted by one layer of fluid on another layer. The shear rate is the rate of change of velocity per unit distance in the direction perpendicular to the flow. 
- Viscosity depends on the temperature, pressure, and composition of the fluid. Generally, viscosity decreases with increasing temperature and decreases with increasing pressure. Viscosity also depends on the molecular structure and interactions of the fluid. For example, fluids with long-chain molecules, such as honey, have higher viscosity than fluids with short-chain molecules, such as water.
- Viscosity can be measured using a viscometer, which is an instrument that measures the flow rate of a fluid through a narrow tube or a gap between two plates. Alternatively, viscosity can be measured using a rotational method, which measures the torque required to rotate a cylinder or a sphere in a fluid.



# A Liquid

- A liquid is one of the three common states of matter, along with solid and gas.
- A liquid is a fluid that can flow and take the shape of its container, but has a constant volume.
- A liquid has a definite surface, unlike a gas, but its surface can change shape due to external forces or gravity.
- A liquid can be compressed slightly, but not as much as a gas.
- A liquid can undergo phase transitions to solid (freezing) or gas (evaporation or boiling) depending on the temperature and pressure.
- A liquid has a higher density than a gas, but a lower density than a solid.
- A liquid can exhibit various properties, such as viscosity, surface tension, capillarity, vapor pressure, and solubility.



# Preparation of Different Resins

Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, insoluble in water, and have a high molecular weight. Resins are widely used in various industries, such as paints, adhesives, plastics, rubber, and pharmaceuticals.

There are different types of resins, such as thermoplastic resins, thermosetting resins, natural resins, and synthetic resins. The preparation of different resins depends on their chemical structure, properties, and applications.

Some of the common methods of preparing different resins are:

- **Polymerization**: This is the process of joining small molecules, called monomers, to form large molecules, called polymers. Polymerization can be initiated by heat, light, catalysts, or radiation. For example, polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, and polyvinyl chloride are prepared by polymerization of ethylene, propylene, styrene, and vinyl chloride monomers, respectively.
- **Polycondensation**: This is the process of forming polymers by the elimination of small molecules, such as water, alcohol, or ammonia, from the reacting monomers. Polycondensation can be carried out in one or more steps, depending on the complexity of the monomers and the desired properties of the polymers. For example, nylon, polyester, and epoxy resins are prepared by polycondensation of diamines and dicarboxylic acids, diols and dicarboxylic acids, and epoxides and amines, respectively.
- **Polyaddition**: This is the process of forming polymers by the addition of monomers without the elimination of any by-products. Polyaddition can be carried out by opening the rings of cyclic monomers, such as epoxides, or by reacting monomers with multiple functional groups, such as isocyanates and polyols. For example, polyurethane and silicone resins are prepared by polyaddition of isocyanates and polyols, and siloxanes and catalysts, respectively.
- **Modification**: This is the process of altering the properties of existing resins by introducing new functional groups, cross-linking agents, fillers, or additives. Modification can improve the mechanical, thermal, electrical, optical, or chemical properties of resins, or impart new functionalities, such as biodegradability, flame retardancy, or antimicrobial activity. For example, phenolic resins can be modified by adding formaldehyde, urea, melamine, or other compounds to form various types of phenol-formaldehyde resins, such as novolac, resol, and bakelite.



# Synthesis of Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid with the formula C6H10O4. It is mainly used for the production of nylon, polyurethanes, and other polymers.
- The conventional method for the synthesis of adipic acid is the oxidation of a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol (KA oil) with nitric acid  .
- The oxidation of KA oil involves several steps and intermediate products, such as cyclohexanone oxime, 6-nitroso-cyclohexanone, 6-nitro-cyclohexanone, and glutaric acid.
- The oxidation of KA oil also generates nitrous oxide (N2O), a greenhouse gas and an ozone-depleting substance, as a by-product .
- The conventional method for the synthesis of adipic acid has several drawbacks, such as high energy consumption, low selectivity, environmental pollution, and safety hazards  .
- Alternative methods for the synthesis of adipic acid have been explored, such as the oxidation of cyclohexene, the hydrocarboxylation of butadiene, the carbonylation of ethylene glycol, and the bioconversion of glucose and its derivatives .
- These alternative methods aim to use renewable and less toxic feedstocks, to reduce the formation of by-products, and to improve the efficiency and selectivity of the reaction .
- The synthesis of adipic acid from glucose and its derivatives is a promising approach, as it can utilize biomass as a renewable and abundant source of carbon.
- However, the synthesis of adipic acid from glucose and its derivatives faces several challenges, such as the low yield, the high cost, the complex reaction pathway, and the design of suitable catalysts.
- The synthesis of adipic acid from glucose and its derivatives requires the development of nanocatalysts that can perform multiple reactions, such as dehydration, hydrogenation, oxidation, and decarboxylation, in a single reactor.
- The nanocatalysts should also have high activity, selectivity, stability, and recyclability, and should be compatible with the reaction conditions and the feedstock properties.
- The synthesis of adipic acid from glucose and its derivatives is an active area of research, and further studies are needed to overcome the existing challenges and to optimize the process parameters.



# Acid and Paracetamol by Conventional and Green Route

- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a common painkiller and antipyretic drug that is derived from phenol.
- The conventional route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves three steps: nitration, reduction and acetylation.
  - In the first step, phenol is nitrated with nitric acid to form p-nitrophenol, which is separated from the unwanted o-nitrophenol by-product by fractional crystallization.
  - In the second step, p-nitrophenol is reduced with tin and hydrochloric acid to form p-aminophenol, which is purified by recrystallization.
  - In the third step, p-aminophenol is acetylated with acetic anhydride to form paracetamol, which is isolated by filtration and recrystallization.
- The conventional route has some drawbacks, such as low yield, high waste generation, use of hazardous reagents and solvents, and formation of undesired isomers and by-products.
- The green route for the synthesis of paracetamol aims to overcome these drawbacks by using alternative methods that are more environmentally friendly, efficient and economical.
- The green route can involve different methods, such as solvent-free synthesis, microwave-assisted synthesis, direct amidation, and enzymatic synthesis.
  - Solvent-free synthesis involves grinding the reactants in a mortar and pestle, and heating them in an oven or a microwave to induce the reaction. This method eliminates the use of solvents and reduces the reaction time and energy consumption.
  - Microwave-assisted synthesis involves irradiating the reactants in a microwave oven, which generates heat and electromagnetic fields that enhance the reaction rate and selectivity. This method also reduces the use of solvents and the reaction time and energy consumption.
  - Direct amidation involves reacting hydroquinone with acetic anhydride in the presence of a catalyst, such as sodium acetate or sodium hydroxide, to form paracetamol directly, without the intermediate steps of nitration and reduction. This method simplifies the synthetic route and avoids the formation of isomers and by-products.
  - Enzymatic synthesis involves using enzymes, such as lipases or nitrilases, to catalyze the reaction between phenol and acetic acid or acetonitrile, respectively, to form paracetamol. This method uses mild reaction conditions, biodegradable reagents, and renewable catalysts.



# List of Experiments

An experiment is a scientific procedure that aims to test a hypothesis, answer a question, or demonstrate a known fact. Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables and measuring their effects on other variables. Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, or outcome.

Some of the common types of experiments are:

- **Controlled experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher controls all the factors that could affect the outcome, except for the independent variable, which is the factor that is being tested. The independent variable is manipulated by the researcher, and the dependent variable is measured by the researcher. The dependent variable is expected to change as a result of the independent variable. A controlled experiment usually has a control group and an experimental group, which are identical in every way except for the independent variable. The control group serves as a baseline for comparison with the experimental group. An example of a controlled experiment is testing the effect of different fertilizers on plant growth.

- **Natural experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher does not manipulate any variables, but observes the effects of a natural event or phenomenon on the variables of interest. The natural event or phenomenon acts as the independent variable, and the dependent variable is measured by the researcher. The researcher cannot control the natural event or phenomenon, but can control other factors that could affect the outcome, such as the selection of the sample, the measurement of the variables, and the analysis of the data. An example of a natural experiment is studying the impact of a volcanic eruption on the climate.

- **Quasi-experiments**: These are experiments where the researcher manipulates the independent variable, but does not have full control over other factors that could affect the outcome, such as the assignment of the participants to the groups, the randomization of the groups, or the presence of confounding variables. Confounding variables are factors that are not the independent variable, but could influence the dependent variable. Quasi-experiments are often used when controlled experiments are not feasible or ethical, such as in social sciences, education, or public health. An example of a quasi-experiment is evaluating the effect of a new curriculum on student achievement.

- **Field experiments**: These are experiments that are conducted in a natural setting, rather than in a laboratory or a controlled environment. Field experiments can be controlled, natural, or quasi-experiments, depending on the degree of control the researcher has over the variables and the setting. Field experiments are often used to study the behavior of humans, animals, or plants in their natural habitats, or to test the applicability of a theory or a method in a real-world context. An example of a field experiment is testing the effectiveness of a new vaccine in a rural community.

- **Laboratory experiments**: These are experiments that are conducted in a controlled environment, such as a laboratory, where the researcher can manipulate the variables and the setting to a high degree. Laboratory experiments can be controlled, natural, or quasi-experiments, depending on the purpose and the design of the experiment. Laboratory experiments are often used to study the basic principles of science, such as physics, chemistry, or biology, or to test the validity of a hypothesis or a model. An example of a laboratory experiment is measuring the speed of light in a vacuum.



# Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

- Calibration is the process of comparing the output of an analytical instrument or device with a known standard of accuracy and adjusting it accordingly.
- Calibration ensures that the instrument or device is working properly and giving reliable and accurate results.
- Calibration is essential for maintaining the quality and validity of analytical data and results.
- Calibration should be performed periodically, before and after each use, or whenever there is a change in the operating conditions or environment of the instrument or device.
- Calibration should be documented and recorded for traceability and verification purposes.
- Calibration can be classified into two types: external and internal calibration.
  - External calibration involves using a reference material or standard solution of known concentration or purity to calibrate the instrument or device. The reference material or standard solution should be similar to the sample being analyzed and have a certified value and uncertainty.
  - Internal calibration involves using an internal standard or a calibration curve to calibrate the instrument or device. The internal standard is a substance that is added to the sample and has a known response or signal. The calibration curve is a plot of the response or signal of the instrument or device versus the concentration or amount of the analyte.
- Some examples of analytical equipment and apparatus that require calibration are:
  - Balances and weighing devices: used to measure the mass or weight of samples and reagents. They should be calibrated using standard weights of known mass and accuracy.
  - Volumetric devices: used to measure or deliver a fixed volume of liquid. They include pipettes, burettes, flasks, cylinders, and syringes. They should be calibrated using distilled water at a specific temperature and pressure.
  - Spectrophotometers: used to measure the absorbance or transmittance of light by a sample. They should be calibrated using blank solutions, reference solutions, or standard curves.
  - pH meters: used to measure the acidity or alkalinity of a solution. They should be calibrated using buffer solutions of known pH and temperature.
  - Thermometers: used to measure the temperature of samples and reagents. They should be calibrated using ice-water baths, boiling water baths, or fixed-point devices.



# Determination of Hardness of Water by EDTA Method

- Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form stable complexes with metal ions, such as Ca2+ and Mg2+.
- The EDTA method is a complexometric titration method that can be used to determine the total hardness or the calcium hardness of water samples.
- The principle of the EDTA method is that when a small amount of a dye such as eriochrome black T (EBT) is added to a water sample at a pH of 10 ± 0.1, the Ca2+ and Mg2+ ions form wine-red colored complexes with EBT. However, when EDTA is added as a titrant, it has a stronger affinity towards Ca2+ and Mg2+ ions and displaces EBT from the complexes, forming colorless EDTA-metal complexes. The end point of the titration is marked by the color change of the solution from wine-red to blue.

- The procedure for the EDTA method is as follows:

  - Take a sample volume of 20 mL (V mL) and dilute it to 40 mL by adding 20 mL of distilled water in an Erlenmeyer flask.
  - Add 1 mL of ammonia buffer to bring the pH to 10 ± 0.1.
  - Add 1 or 2 drops of EBT indicator solution.
  - Titrate the sample with EDTA solution of known concentration (C mg/mL) with vigorous shaking until the wine-red color just turns blue.
  - Record the volume of EDTA solution used (T mL).

- The calculation of the hardness of water by EDTA method is based on the following formula:

  - Hardness (mg/L as CaCO3) = (C × T × 1000) / V

  - Where C is the concentration of EDTA solution in mg/mL, T is the volume of EDTA solution used in mL, and V is the volume of water sample taken in mL.

  - The hardness can be reported as total hardness or calcium hardness, depending on whether the water sample contains only calcium ions or both calcium and magnesium ions.



# Determination of Alkalinity of Water Sample

- Alkalinity is the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, sodium, and potassium in water.
- Alkalinity is important for water quality because it affects the pH, corrosion, and disinfection processes. High alkalinity can cause scaling and reduce the effectiveness of chlorine. Low alkalinity can cause corrosion and pH fluctuations.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange, to detect the endpoint of the titration.
- The endpoint of the titration depends on the type and concentration of the alkaline species in the water sample. There are three types of alkalinity: phenolphthalein alkalinity (P), total alkalinity (T), and hydroxide alkalinity (OH).
- Phenolphthalein alkalinity is the amount of acid required to lower the pH of the water sample to 8.3, where phenolphthalein changes from pink to colorless. It represents the sum of hydroxide and half of carbonate alkalinity.
- Total alkalinity is the amount of acid required to lower the pH of the water sample to 4.5, where methyl orange changes from yellow to orange. It represents the sum of hydroxide, carbonate, and bicarbonate alkalinity.
- Hydroxide alkalinity is the amount of acid required to lower the pH of the water sample from 8.3 to 10.0, where phenolphthalein changes from colorless to pink again. It represents the hydroxide alkalinity only.
- The following equations can be used to calculate the different types of alkalinity from the titration results:

  - P = V1 x N x 50 / ml of sample
  - T = V2 x N x 50 / ml of sample
  - OH = 2P - T

  where V1 is the volume of acid used to reach the first endpoint, V2 is the volume of acid used to reach the second endpoint, N is the normality of the acid, and ml of sample is the volume of the water sample. The units of alkalinity are mg/L as CaCO3.



# Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte).
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H+].
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a pH meter to monitor the change in pH of the analyte solution as the titrant is added gradually.
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that responds to the hydrogen ion activity in the solution, and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential. The difference in potential between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution.
- A plot of the pH of the analyte solution versus the volume of the titrant added is called a titration curve. The titration curve can be used to determine the endpoint or equivalence point of the titration, where the analyte and the titrant are stoichiometrically equivalent.
- The shape and position of the titration curve depend on the nature and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration, as well as the buffer capacity of the solution.
- The buffer capacity is a measure of the ability of the solution to resist changes in pH when small amounts of acid or base are added. It is related to the concentrations and dissociation constants of the acid-base pairs in the solution.
- The titration curve can also be used to calculate the pKa or pKb of a weak acid or base, by finding the pH at the half-equivalence point, where the concentration of the weak acid or base is equal to the concentration of its conjugate base or acid. At this point, the pH is equal to the pKa or pKb of the weak acid or base.



# 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is the property of a liquid that makes its surface behave like a stretched elastic membrane.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the stalagmometer method.
- In this experiment, we will use the stalagmometer method to determine the surface tension of a given liquid.
- A stalagmometer is a device that consists of a glass tube with a bulb at one end and a narrow opening at the other end.
- The stalagmometer is filled with the liquid to be tested and then inverted over a graduated cylinder.
- The liquid drops from the narrow opening of the stalagmometer and the number of drops and the volume of liquid collected in the graduated cylinder are noted.
- The surface tension of the liquid is given by the formula:

$$\gamma = \frac{4\pi r^3 \rho g}{3V}$$

where $\gamma$ is the surface tension, $r$ is the radius of the opening of the stalagmometer, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity, and $V$ is the volume of liquid corresponding to one drop.

- To perform the experiment, we need the following apparatus:

  - A stalagmometer
  - A graduated cylinder
  - A thermometer
  - A stopwatch
  - A balance
  - A liquid to be tested (such as water, alcohol, glycerin, etc.)
  - A liquid of known surface tension (such as distilled water)

- The procedure of the experiment is as follows:

  - Clean and dry the stalagmometer and the graduated cylinder.
  - Fill the stalagmometer with the liquid of known surface tension (such as distilled water) up to the bulb.
  - Invert the stalagmometer over the graduated cylinder and allow the liquid to drop from the narrow opening.
  - Start the stopwatch and count the number of drops until a certain volume of liquid (such as 10 mL) is collected in the graduated cylinder.
  - Stop the stopwatch and note the time taken and the volume of liquid collected.
  - Repeat the above steps for at least three trials and calculate the average volume of liquid corresponding to one drop ($V_1$).
  - Repeat the above steps with the liquid to be tested and calculate the average volume of liquid corresponding to one drop ($V_2$).
  - Measure the temperature of both liquids and note it down.
  - Weigh the stalagmometer when empty and when filled with the liquid to be tested and calculate the mass of the liquid ($m$).
  - Calculate the density of the liquid ($\rho$) by dividing the mass by the volume of the stalagmometer ($V_s$).
  - Measure the radius of the opening of the stalagmometer ($r$) by using a magnifying glass and a scale.
  - Calculate the surface tension of the liquid ($\gamma_2$) by using the formula:

  $$\gamma_2 = \frac{V_1}{V_2} \gamma_1$$

  where $\gamma_1$ is the surface tension of the liquid of known surface tension (such as distilled water).

  - Record the observations and calculations in a tabular form and report the result with proper units and significant figures.



# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied shear stress.
- Viscosity is important for many applications, such as lubrication, fluid dynamics, transport phenomena, etc.
- A viscometer is an instrument that measures the viscosity of a fluid by measuring the force or torque required to move a surface through the fluid at a constant speed or the speed at which a surface moves through the fluid under a constant force or torque.
- There are different types of viscometers, such as capillary, rotational, falling ball, etc. Each type has its own advantages and limitations depending on the fluid properties and the measurement conditions.
- In this experiment, we will use a capillary viscometer, which consists of a glass tube with a narrow section (the capillary) and two marks on the tube to indicate the fluid level. The fluid is drawn into the tube by suction and then allowed to flow out under its own weight. The time taken for the fluid to flow between the two marks is measured and used to calculate the viscosity of the fluid.
- The capillary viscometer obeys the Hagen-Poiseuille equation, which relates the volumetric flow rate Q of a fluid through a capillary of length L and radius R to the pressure difference P across the capillary and the viscosity η of the fluid:

  Q = (πR^4 P)/(8ηL)

- Rearranging the equation, we can obtain the expression for the viscosity of the fluid:

  η = (πR^4 P)/(8QL)

- The pressure difference P can be approximated by the hydrostatic pressure due to the height difference h of the fluid column between the two marks:

  P = ρgh

  where ρ is the density of the fluid and g is the acceleration due to gravity.

- The volumetric flow rate Q can be obtained by dividing the volume V of the fluid that flows between the two marks by the time t taken for the flow:

  Q = V/t

- Substituting these expressions into the equation for the viscosity, we get:

  η = (πR^4 ρgh)/(8Vt)

- To perform the experiment, we need to know the following parameters:

  - The radius R of the capillary, which can be measured by a micrometer or a vernier caliper.
  - The length L of the capillary, which can be measured by a ruler or a meter stick.
  - The volume V of the fluid that flows between the two marks, which can be calculated by multiplying the cross-sectional area A of the tube by the distance d between the two marks:

    V = Ad

    where A = πR^2

  - The density ρ of the fluid, which can be measured by a hydrometer or a pycnometer.
  - The time t taken for the fluid to flow between the two marks, which can be measured by a stopwatch or a chronometer.
  - The height difference h of the fluid column between the two marks, which can be measured by a ruler or a meter stick.

- The procedure of the experiment is as follows:

  - Clean and dry the viscometer and the fluid container.
  - Fill the fluid container with the given liquid and place it on a stand.
  - Connect the viscometer to the fluid container by a rubber tube and a suction pump.
  - Draw the fluid into the viscometer by suction until it reaches above the upper mark.
  - Close the suction pump and disconnect the rubber tube.
  - Hold the viscometer vertically and start the stopwatch when the fluid level reaches the upper mark.
  - Stop the stopwatch when the fluid level reaches the lower mark and record the time t.
  - Repeat the steps 4 to 7 for at least three trials and take the average time t.
  - Measure the radius R, the length L, the distance d, and the height difference h of the viscometer and record the values.
  - Measure the density ρ of the fluid and record the value.
  - Calculate the volume V of the fluid that flows between the two marks using the formula V = Ad.
  - Calculate the viscosity η of the fluid using the formula η = (πR^4 ρgh)/(8Vt) and record the value.
  - Report the result with the appropriate units and significant figures.

- The sources of error and uncertainty in this experiment include:

  - The parallax error in reading the fluid level and the height difference on the viscometer.
  - The human error in starting and



# Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a light green crystalline salt with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2.6H2O. It is used as a reducing agent in redox titrations.
- An external indicator is a substance that changes color when added to the titration flask after the end point has been reached. It is used to confirm the end point of the titration and to avoid over-titration.
- One example of an external indicator is potassium ferricyanide, which forms a blue precipitate of Prussian blue with excess ferrous ions after the end point of the titration with potassium dichromate.
- The procedure for determining the strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of potassium dichromate (K2Cr2O7) of known concentration (e.g. 0.025 M) by dissolving a weighed amount of the salt in distilled water and making up the volume in a volumetric flask.
  - Pipette out a known volume (e.g. 10 mL) of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution of unknown concentration into a conical flask and add some dilute sulfuric acid (H2SO4) to prevent the hydrolysis of ferrous ions.
  - Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution with the standard potassium dichromate solution using a burette until a faint green color is observed in the flask. This indicates that all the ferrous ions have been oxidized to ferric ions by the dichromate ions. Record the volume of the potassium dichromate solution used as V1.
  - Add a few drops of the external indicator (potassium ferricyanide) to the flask and swirl. If a blue color appears, it means that some ferrous ions are still present and the titration is not complete. Add more potassium dichromate solution dropwise until the blue color disappears. Record the final volume of the potassium dichromate solution used as V2.
  - Repeat the titration with another aliquot of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution and take the average of V2 as the final volume of the potassium dichromate solution required for the titration.
  - Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution using the following formula:

    - Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution = (V2 x N2 x M2) / (V1 x M1)

    - Where V2 is the final volume of the potassium dichromate solution in mL, N2 is the normality of the potassium dichromate solution, M2 is the molar mass of ferrous ammonium sulfate (392.14 g/mol), V1 is the volume of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution in mL, and M1 is the molarity of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution.



# Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of an unknown solution of a reducing agent, such as ferrous ammonium sulphate.
- The reaction between potassium dichromate and ferrous ammonium sulphate is a redox reaction, in which potassium dichromate acts as an oxidizing agent and ferrous ammonium sulphate acts as a reducing agent.
- The reaction can be represented by the following equation:

`6FeSO4(NH4)2SO4.6H2O + K2Cr2O7 + 7H2SO4 -> 3Fe2(SO4)3 + Cr2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + 24H2O + 6NH4`

- The end point of the titration can be detected by the change in color of the solution, from green to reddish-brown, due to the formation of chromium(III) sulphate. This is an example of an internal indicator, which is a substance that changes color as a result of the reaction itself.
- The strength of potassium dichromate can be calculated by using the following formula:

`Strength of K2Cr2O7 = (Strength of FAS x Volume of FAS x 1000) / (Volume of K2Cr2O7 x 49.04)`

- Where FAS stands for ferrous ammonium sulphate, and 49.04 is the equivalent weight of potassium dichromate.



# 9. Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder

- Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite (Ca(OCl)2), calcium chloride (CaCl2), and calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2).
- Available chlorine is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated from bleaching powder by the action of acids.
- Available chlorine is expressed as a percentage of the mass of bleaching powder.
- The determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder involves the following steps:

  - A known mass of bleaching powder is dissolved in water and filtered to remove insoluble impurities.
  - A measured volume of the filtrate is titrated with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3) using starch as an indicator.
  - The reaction involved is:

    Ca(OCl)2 + 2Na2S2O3 + 2H2O -> CaSO4 + 2Na2SO4 + 2NaHSO3 + H2O

  - The end point is marked by the disappearance of the blue color of the starch-iodine complex.
  - The volume of sodium thiosulfate used is noted and the percentage of available chlorine is calculated using the formula:

    % available chlorine = (V x N x 35.5 x 100) / (W x 1000)

    where V is the volume of sodium thiosulfate in mL, N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate, W is the mass of bleaching powder in g, and 35.5 is the equivalent mass of chlorine.



# 10. Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride is one of the major anions found in natural waters. It originates from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage.
- Chloride can affect the taste, corrosion, and biological activity of water. Therefore, it is important to monitor and control its concentration in drinking water, wastewater, and environmental samples.
- The most common method for determining chloride content in water sample is the **argentometric method**. It is based on the precipitation of chloride ions as silver chloride by adding a known excess of silver nitrate solution and then titrating the excess silver ions with a standard solution of sodium or potassium thiocyanate using ferric ammonium sulfate as an indicator.
- The following steps describe the procedure of the argentometric method:

  1. Pipette a 50 mL aliquot of the water sample into a 250 mL conical flask and add 2 mL of 6 M nitric acid.
  2. Add 2 mL of 0.1% potassium chromate solution as an indicator. A yellow precipitate of silver chromate may form, indicating the presence of chloride ions.
  3. Titrate the sample with 0.02 M silver nitrate solution from a burette until a faint reddish-brown color appears, indicating the end point. Record the volume of silver nitrate solution used as V1.
  4. Add 2 mL of 10% ferric ammonium sulfate solution and continue the titration with 0.1 M sodium or potassium thiocyanate solution until a permanent brownish-red color appears, indicating the end point. Record the volume of thiocyanate solution used as V2.
  5. Repeat the procedure with a blank sample of distilled water and record the volumes of silver nitrate and thiocyanate solutions used as V1' and V2', respectively.
  6. Calculate the chloride content in the water sample using the following formula:

     Cl- (mg/L) = (V1 - V1') x 0.02 x 35.45 x 1000 / 50 - (V2 - V2') x 0.1 x 58.5 x 1000 / 50

     where 35.45 is the molar mass of chloride ion and 58.5 is the molar mass of thiocyanate ion.

- The following table shows an example of the calculation:

| Sample | V1 (mL) | V1' (mL) | V2 (mL) | V2' (mL) | Cl- (mg/L) |
|--------|---------|----------|---------|----------|------------|
| Water  | 12.5    | 0.5      | 3.0     | 0.2      | 8.75       |



# Preparation of Phenol Formaldehyde (PF) Resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the reaction of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol formaldehyde resin can be prepared by a step-growth polymerization reaction that can be either acid- or base-catalyzed .
- The ratio of phenol to formaldehyde affects the structure and properties of the resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio of formaldehyde produces cross-linked networks.
- The steps involved in the preparation of phenol formaldehyde resin are :

  - Weighing the appropriate quantity of phenol and formaldehyde and mixing them in a reaction vessel.
  - Adding a catalyst, such as hydrochloric acid, sulfuric acid, or ammonia, to initiate the polymerization reaction.
  - Heating the mixture to a suitable temperature and controlling the reaction time to achieve the desired degree of polymerization and viscosity.
  - Cooling and neutralizing the resin with an alkali or an acid, depending on the catalyst used.
  - Filtering and drying the resin to remove any impurities and excess water.
  - Molding, curing, or modifying the resin for various applications.



# Preparation of Urea Formaldehyde (UF) Resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is synthesized by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst .
- The reaction can be carried out in one, two, or three steps, depending on the desired properties and applications of the resin .
- In the one-step process, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:1.5 to 2.5 and heated at 80-100°C with a base catalyst (such as ammonia or pyridine) until the desired viscosity is reached . The resin is then neutralized with an acid (such as sulfuric acid or phosphoric acid) and stored at low temperature . This process produces a resin with a high formaldehyde content and a low molecular weight, which is suitable for low-pressure applications such as paper impregnation and textile finishing .
- In the two-step process, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:2 to 3 and heated at 80-100°C with a base catalyst until a low molecular weight precondensate is formed . The precondensate is then cooled and mixed with more urea and formaldehyde in a molar ratio of 1:0.5 to 1.5 and heated again at 80-100°C with an acid catalyst (such as oxalic acid or hydrochloric acid) until the desired viscosity is reached . The resin is then neutralized with a base and stored at low temperature . This process produces a resin with a lower formaldehyde content and a higher molecular weight, which is suitable for high-pressure applications such as plywood and particle board .
- In the three-step process, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:2 to 3 and heated at 80-100°C with a base catalyst until a low molecular weight precondensate is formed . The precondensate is then cooled and mixed with more urea and formaldehyde in a molar ratio of 1:0.5 to 1.5 and heated again at 80-100°C with a base catalyst until a medium molecular weight precondensate is formed . The precondensate is then cooled and mixed with more urea and formaldehyde in a molar ratio of 1:0.5 to 1.5 and heated again at 80-100°C with an acid catalyst until the desired viscosity is reached . The resin is then neutralized with a base and stored at low temperature . This process produces a resin with a very low formaldehyde content and a very high molecular weight, which is suitable for high-performance applications such as MDF and molded objects .
- The structure of UF resin is composed of linear and branched chains of methylene-bridged urea units, with some methylol groups attached to the nitrogen atoms. The degree of polymerization and cross-linking depends on the reaction conditions and the molar ratio of urea and formaldehyde. The curing of UF resin involves the elimination of water and the formation of methylene and dimethylene ether linkages between the methylol groups.
- The properties of UF resin depend on the composition, molecular weight, degree of cross-linking, and curing conditions. Some of the advantages of UF resin are its low cost, high strength, high hardness, high thermal stability, and good chemical resistance. Some of the disadvantages of UF resin are its poor moisture resistance, poor weathering resistance, poor dimensional stability, and high formaldehyde emission.
- UF resin can be modified by adding various additives, such as fillers, extenders, plasticizers, stabilizers, catalysts, and modifiers, to improve its properties and performance. Some of



# Preparation of Adipic acid / Paracetamol

- Adipic acid is an organic compound with the formula (CH2)4(COOH)2. It is the most important dicarboxylic acid, mainly used as a precursor for the production of nylon.
- Paracetamol is an organic compound with the formula C8H9NO2. It is a widely used antipyretic and analgesic drug.

## Preparation of Adipic acid

- Adipic acid is produced from a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol called KA oil, the abbreviation of ketone-alcohol oil.
- The KA oil is oxidized with nitric acid to give adipic acid, via a multistep pathway .
- The overall reaction is:

adipic acid synthesis

- The main steps are:

  - Nitration of KA oil to form a mixture of nitro compounds.
  - Oxidation of the nitro compounds to form a mixture of carboxylic acids.
  - Hydrolysis of the carboxylic acids to form adipic acid and other by-products.

## Preparation of Paracetamol

- Paracetamol is prepared from p-aminophenol by acetylating it with acetic anhydride in the presence of 3-4 drops of concentrated sulphuric acid as catalyst .
- The reaction is:

paracetamol synthesis

- The mechanism is:

  - Protonation of the oxygen atom of acetic anhydride by sulphuric acid.
  - Nucleophilic attack of the nitrogen atom of p-aminophenol on the carbonyl carbon of acetic anhydride.
  - Elimination of acetic acid and formation of paracetamol.

- Paracetamol can also be prepared from phenol by a three-step synthesis involving nitration, reduction and acetylation.



# Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- Cell conductance of a solution is the reciprocal of its resistance, which is the opposition to the flow of electric current through the solution.
- Cell conductance of a solution depends on the concentration, charge, and mobility of the ions present in the solution.
- Cell conductance of a solution can be measured by using a special type of cell known as a conductivity cell and a Wheatstone bridge.
- A conductivity cell consists of two electrodes of known area and distance, which are immersed in the solution whose conductance is to be measured.
- A Wheatstone bridge is a circuit that can measure the unknown resistance of a solution by comparing it with a known resistance.
- The cell constant of a conductivity cell is the ratio of the distance between the electrodes to the area of cross-section of the electrodes, and it has the units of m^-1^.
- The cell constant can be determined by measuring the resistance of a cell filled with a solution of known conductivity, such as KCl.
- The conductivity of any solution can be calculated from the experimental resistance and the cell constant by using the formula:

  `conductivity = cell constant / resistance`

- The conductivity of a solution is expressed in units of S m^-1^ or mS cm^-1^, where S is the symbol for siemens, the unit of conductance.



# Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Esters are organic compounds that have the general formula RCOOR', where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.
- Esters can be hydrolyzed by water in the presence of an acid or a base to form carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- The hydrolysis of esters is a first-order reaction, which means that the rate of the reaction depends only on the concentration of the ester.
- The rate constant of the hydrolysis of esters can be determined by measuring the change in the concentration of the ester or the acid or the alcohol over time.
- One method to determine the rate constant of the hydrolysis of esters is to use a conductometric titration, which measures the change in the electrical conductivity of the solution as the reaction proceeds.
- The conductometric titration involves adding a known volume of a standard base solution (such as sodium hydroxide) to a known volume of the ester solution (such as ethyl acetate) in the presence of an excess of a strong acid (such as hydrochloric acid).
- The strong acid catalyzes the hydrolysis of the ester and also neutralizes the base that is added.
- The conductivity of the solution decreases as the base is added, because the ions of the base are consumed by the acid and the ester.
- The conductivity reaches a minimum when the base is equivalent to the acid, and then increases again as the base is in excess and hydrolyzes the remaining ester.
- The rate constant of the hydrolysis of the ester can be calculated from the slope of the plot of the logarithm of the conductivity versus time, using the following equation:

  - k = -2.303 * (slope) / (V1 + V2)

  - where k is the rate constant, V1 is the volume of the ester solution, and V2 is the volume of the base solution.



# Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Organic compounds are those that contain carbon atoms, usually bonded to hydrogen, oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, or halogens.
- Functional groups are specific groups of atoms or bonds within organic molecules that are responsible for their chemical properties and reactions.
- Element detection is the process of identifying the presence of certain elements in an organic compound, such as carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, sulfur, halogens, etc.
- Identification of functional groups is the process of determining the type and location of functional groups in an organic compound, such as alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, amines, etc.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups are important steps in the qualitative analysis of organic compounds, which helps to determine their structure and properties.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups can be done by various chemical tests, such as:

  - Carbon and hydrogen detection: A small amount of the organic compound is heated with copper oxide in a test tube. If carbon and hydrogen are present, carbon dioxide and water are formed, which can be detected by passing the gas through lime water (turns milky) and anhydrous copper sulfate (turns blue).
  - Nitrogen detection: A small amount of the organic compound is heated with sodium metal in a test tube. If nitrogen is present, ammonia gas is evolved, which can be detected by its pungent smell and by turning moist red litmus paper blue.
  - Sulfur detection: A small amount of the organic compound is heated with sodium metal in a test tube. If sulfur is present, hydrogen sulfide gas is evolved, which can be detected by its rotten egg smell and by turning moist lead acetate paper black.
  - Halogen detection: A small amount of the organic compound is heated with sodium metal in a test tube. If halogens are present, sodium halides are formed, which can be detected by adding dilute nitric acid and silver nitrate solution. A white precipitate indicates chloride, a cream precipitate indicates bromide, and a yellow precipitate indicates iodide.
  - Alcoholic -OH group detection: A small amount of the organic compound is added to a solution of ceric ammonium nitrate. If an alcoholic -OH group is present, the solution turns red.
  - Carbonyl group (aldehydes and ketones) detection: A small amount of the organic compound is added to a solution of 2,4-dinitrophenylhydrazine. If a carbonyl group is present, a yellow, orange, or red precipitate of the corresponding hydrazone is formed.
  - Aldehyde group detection: A small amount of the organic compound is added to a solution of Fehling's reagent (a mixture of copper sulfate, sodium carbonate, and potassium sodium tartrate). If an aldehyde group is present, a red precipitate of copper oxide is formed. Alternatively, a small amount of the organic compound is added to a solution of Tollens' reagent (a mixture of silver nitrate and ammonia). If an aldehyde group is present, a silver mirror is formed on the inner surface of the test tube.
  - Carboxylic acid group detection: A small amount of the organic compound is added to a solution of sodium bicarbonate. If a carboxylic acid group is present, carbon dioxide gas is evolved, which can be detected by its effervescence and by turning lime water milky.
  - Amino group detection: A small amount of the organic compound is added to a solution of ninhydrin. If a primary or secondary amino group is present, a purple color is produced. Alternatively, a small amount of the organic compound is added to a solution of nitrous acid. If a primary amino group is present, nitrogen gas is evolved, which can be detected by its bubbling and by displacing water in an inverted test tube. If a secondary amino group is present, a yellow oily liquid of nitrosamine is formed.
