# Water

- Water is a chemical compound with the formula H2O, meaning that each of its molecules contains one oxygen and two hydrogen atoms connected by covalent bonds.
- Water is the most abundant substance on Earth, covering about 71% of its surface. It is also the only natural substance that exists in all three states of matter: solid (ice), liquid (water), and gas (water vapor).
- Water is essential for life, as it is involved in many biological processes such as metabolism, transport, and regulation. Water also acts as a solvent, dissolving many substances and facilitating chemical reactions.
- Water has many unique physical and chemical properties that make it important for various natural and human-made phenomena. Some of these properties are:
  - High specific heat capacity: Water can absorb or release a large amount of heat without changing its temperature significantly, which helps moderate the climate and maintain a stable body temperature.
  - High heat of vaporization: Water requires a lot of energy to change from liquid to gas, which allows water to evaporate and cool the surface, and also to form clouds and precipitation.
  - High surface tension: Water molecules are strongly attracted to each other, creating a thin layer of water on the surface that resists external forces. This enables water to form droplets, bubbles, and waves, and also allows some organisms to walk or float on water.
  - Universal solvent: Water can dissolve many polar and ionic substances, such as salts, sugars, acids, and bases, making water a medium for transporting nutrients, wastes, and signals in living systems and the environment.
  - Density anomaly: Water has its maximum density at 4°C, which means that ice is less dense than liquid water and floats on it. This prevents water from freezing completely in cold regions and preserves aquatic life.



# 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a solution is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. pH = -log[H+]
- The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, with 7 being neutral, lower than 7 being acidic, and higher than 7 being basic. The pH of pure water is 7 at 25°C.
- pH can be measured using indicators, such as litmus paper or phenolphthalein, which change color depending on the acidity or basicity of the solution. Alternatively, pH can be measured using a pH meter, which is an electronic device that measures the voltage difference between a reference electrode and a pH-sensitive electrode immersed in the solution.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is defined as the force per unit length required to stretch or break the surface of a liquid. Surface tension = F/L
- Surface tension causes liquids to form spherical droplets, to minimize their surface area. It also allows some insects, such as water striders, to walk on the surface of water without sinking.
- Surface tension can be measured using methods such as the drop weight method, the ring method, or the pendant drop method, which involve measuring the weight, force, or shape of a liquid drop hanging from a solid surface or a needle.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a fluid is to flow. It is defined as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in a fluid. Viscosity = τ/γ
- The shear stress is the force per unit area applied parallel to the surface of a fluid layer. The shear rate is the rate of change of velocity per unit distance perpendicular to the surface of a fluid layer.
- Viscosity depends on the temperature, pressure, and composition of the fluid. Generally, viscosity decreases with increasing temperature and decreases with increasing pressure. Viscosity also varies with the type and concentration of solutes or suspended particles in the fluid.
- Viscosity can be measured using methods such as the capillary tube method, the rotating cylinder method, or the falling ball method, which involve measuring the flow rate, torque, or terminal velocity of a fluid through a narrow tube, between two cylinders, or around a sphere, respectively.



# A Liquid

- A liquid is one of the four states of matter, along with solid, gas, and plasma.
- A liquid is a fluid that has a definite volume but no fixed shape. It can flow and take the shape of its container.
- A liquid is composed of molecules that are loosely bound together by intermolecular forces, such as hydrogen bonds or van der Waals forces.
- A liquid can change its state to a solid by freezing, to a gas by boiling or evaporating, or to a plasma by ionizing.
- Some examples of liquids are water, oil, mercury, blood, and alcohol.



# Preparation of Different Resins

- Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, insoluble in water, and have a high molecular weight.
- Resins are widely used in various industries, such as plastics, paints, adhesives, coatings, and cosmetics.
- Resins can be classified into two main types: thermoplastic and thermosetting.
- Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be melted and reshaped by heating, such as polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, and nylon.
- Thermosetting resins are resins that undergo irreversible chemical reactions when heated, forming cross-linked networks that cannot be remelted, such as epoxy, phenolic, and polyester resins.
- Resins can be prepared by different methods, depending on their type and properties. Some of the common methods are:

  - Polymerization: This is the process of joining small molecules (monomers) to form large molecules (polymers) with repeating units. Polymerization can be initiated by heat, light, catalysts, or other agents. For example, polyethylene is prepared by polymerizing ethylene gas under high pressure and temperature in the presence of a catalyst.
  - Condensation: This is the process of combining two or more molecules with the elimination of a small molecule, such as water, alcohol, or ammonia. Condensation can be carried out by heating, acid or base catalysis, or dehydration agents. For example, phenolic resin is prepared by condensing phenol and formaldehyde in the presence of an acid catalyst.
  - Esterification: This is the process of forming an ester bond between a carboxylic acid and an alcohol, with the elimination of water. Esterification can be catalyzed by acids, bases, or enzymes. For example, polyester resin is prepared by esterifying dicarboxylic acids and diols in the presence of an acid catalyst.
  - Addition: This is the process of adding one or more molecules to a double or triple bond of another molecule, without the elimination of any atoms. Addition can be facilitated by heat, light, catalysts, or free radicals. For example, polyvinyl chloride (PVC) is prepared by adding chlorine to ethylene under the influence of light or a free radical initiator.



# Synthesis of Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is an organic compound with the formula (CH2)4(COOH)2. It is a white crystalline solid that is used as a precursor for the production of nylon and other polymers.
- Adipic acid can be synthesized from various starting materials, such as cyclohexane, cyclohexene, cyclohexanol, or benzene. Some of the common methods are:

  - Oxidation of cyclohexane with nitric acid or air in the presence of a catalyst. This is an industrial process that produces large amounts of adipic acid and nitrous oxide, a greenhouse gas.
  - Oxidation of cyclohexene with hydrogen peroxide and a tungsten catalyst. This is a green and selective method that avoids the formation of nitrous oxide and other byproducts.
  - Oxidation of cyclohexanol with nitric acid or potassium permanganate. This is a laboratory method that requires high temperatures and pressures and yields a mixture of adipic acid and glutaric acid.
  - Oxidation of benzene with nitric acid and a vanadium catalyst. This is a historical method that was used before the discovery of the cyclohexane route. It involves the formation of nitrobenzene, phenol, and maleic anhydride as intermediates.

- Adipic acid can also be synthesized from renewable sources, such as glucose, fatty acids, or lignin. Some of the emerging methods are:

  - Biocatalytic conversion of glucose to adipic acid using engineered microorganisms or enzymes. This is a sustainable and environmentally friendly method that mimics the natural metabolic pathways of living cells.
  - Hydrogenation of muconic acid, a derivative of glucose, using a ruthenium catalyst. This is a simple and efficient method that can be performed under mild conditions and produces high yields of adipic acid.
  - Oxidative cleavage of fatty acids or lignin using ozone or oxygen in the presence of a metal catalyst. This is a versatile and selective method that can utilize various biomass feedstocks and generate adipic acid and other valuable chemicals.



# Acid and Paracetamol by Conventional and Green Route

- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a common painkiller and antipyretic drug that is derived from phenol.
- The conventional route for the synthesis of paracetamol involves three steps: nitration, reduction and acetylation.
  - In the first step, phenol is nitrated with nitric acid to form p-nitrophenol, which is separated from the unwanted o-nitrophenol by steam distillation.
  - In the second step, p-nitrophenol is reduced with tin and hydrochloric acid to form p-aminophenol, which is purified by recrystallization.
  - In the third step, p-aminophenol is acetylated with acetic anhydride to form paracetamol, which is isolated by filtration and recrystallization.
- The conventional route has some drawbacks, such as low yield, high waste generation, use of hazardous reagents and solvents, and formation of by-products and impurities.
- The green route for the synthesis of paracetamol aims to overcome these drawbacks by using alternative methods that are more environmentally friendly, efficient and economical.
- The green route can involve different methods, such as solvent-free synthesis, microwave-assisted synthesis, direct amidation and enzymatic synthesis.
  - Solvent-free synthesis involves the use of solid reagents or catalysts without any solvent, which reduces the waste and energy consumption. For example, paracetamol can be synthesized from p-nitrophenol and acetic anhydride in the presence of a solid acid catalyst under microwave irradiation.
  - Microwave-assisted synthesis involves the use of microwave energy to heat the reaction mixture, which enhances the reaction rate and yield. For example, paracetamol can be synthesized from phenol and nitric acid under microwave irradiation, followed by reduction and acetylation.
  - Direct amidation involves the use of a carboxylic acid instead of an acylating agent, which avoids the formation of acetic acid as a by-product. For example, paracetamol can be synthesized from hydroquinone and acetic acid in the presence of a base catalyst under microwave irradiation.
  - Enzymatic synthesis involves the use of enzymes as biocatalysts, which are selective, mild and biodegradable. For example, paracetamol can be synthesized from p-aminophenol and acetic acid using lipase as an enzyme under mild conditions.
- The green route has some advantages, such as high yield, low waste generation, use of benign reagents and solvents, and avoidance of by-products and impurities.



# List of Experiments

- An experiment is a procedure that tests a hypothesis or a question by collecting and analyzing data under controlled conditions.
- Experiments are used to test the effects of one or more factors (called independent variables) on one or more outcomes (called dependent variables).
- Experiments can be classified into different types based on their design, purpose, and level of control.
- Some common types of experiments are:

  - **Randomized controlled trial (RCT)**: An experiment in which participants are randomly assigned to either a treatment group or a control group, and the outcomes are compared between the groups. RCTs are considered the gold standard for testing the effectiveness of interventions in medicine, education, and social sciences.
  - **Factorial design**: An experiment in which two or more independent variables are manipulated simultaneously, and their effects on the dependent variable are measured. Factorial designs can test for interactions between the independent variables, as well as their main effects.
  - **Quasi-experiment**: An experiment in which the participants are not randomly assigned to the groups, but the groups are formed based on some existing characteristic or condition. Quasi-experiments are often used when randomization is not possible or ethical, but they have lower internal validity than RCTs.
  - **Natural experiment**: An experiment in which the independent variable is manipulated by a natural event or phenomenon, rather than by the researcher. Natural experiments can provide causal evidence in situations where controlled experiments are not feasible or ethical, but they have lower external validity than RCTs.
  - **Observational study**: A study in which the researcher does not manipulate any variable, but only observes and measures the existing relationships between variables. Observational studies can describe patterns and associations, but they cannot establish causality.



# Calibration of Analytical Equipment and Apparatus

- Calibration is the process of evaluating and adjusting the precision and accuracy of measurement equipment.
- Calibration ensures that the equipment and apparatus are functioning properly and producing valid data for future reference.
- Calibration also helps to maintain a safe working environment and comply with quality standards and regulations.
- Calibration involves comparing the output of the equipment or apparatus with a known reference standard and correcting any deviation or error.
- Calibration should be performed at regular intervals, depending on the frequency of use, the stability of the equipment, the manufacturer's recommendations, and the in-house standard operating procedures .
- Calibration should also be performed whenever there is a change in the environmental conditions, the equipment is repaired or modified, or the equipment is suspected of malfunctioning.
- Calibration should be documented and recorded, including the date, the reference standard, the calibration method, the results, and the corrective actions if any.
- Calibration should be done by qualified and trained personnel, using appropriate and traceable reference standards and methods .



# Determination of Hardness of Water by EDTA Method

- Hardness of water is a measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form complexes with metal ions such as Ca2+ and Mg2+.
- EDTA titration is a method of determining the hardness of water by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of EDTA.
- The indicator used in EDTA titration is Eriochrome Black T (EBT), which is a dye that changes color from wine red to blue when it binds to metal ions.
- The pH of the water sample must be adjusted to 10 ± 0.1 using ammonia buffer to ensure that only Ca2+ and Mg2+ ions react with EDTA and EBT.

## Procedure for Determination of Hardness of Water by EDTA Titration

1. Take a sample volume of 20 mL (V mL) of water in a clean Erlenmeyer flask.
2. Dilute the sample to 40 mL by adding 20 mL of distilled water.
3. Add 1 mL of ammonia buffer to bring the pH to 10 ± 0.1.
4. Add 1 or 2 drops of EBT indicator solution. The sample should turn wine red due to the presence of Ca2+ and Mg2+ ions.
5. Titrate the sample with a standard EDTA solution of known concentration (C mg/mL) from a burette until the color changes from wine red to blue. Note the final burette reading (B mL).
6. Repeat the titration with two more samples and take the average burette reading (A mL).

## Calculation of Hardness of Water by EDTA Titration

- The hardness of water (H mg/L) can be calculated by the following formula:

H = (A x C x 1000) / V

where A is the average burette reading (mL), C is the concentration of EDTA solution (mg/mL), and V is the sample volume (mL).

- The hardness of water can be expressed in terms of CaCO3 equivalent, which is the mass of CaCO3 that would produce the same amount of hardness as the water sample.

- The conversion factor between CaCO3 equivalent and EDTA is 1 mg of CaCO3 = 1 mg of EDTA.

- Therefore, the hardness of water in terms of CaCO3 equivalent (H mg/L as CaCO3) is equal to the hardness of water in terms of EDTA (H mg/L).

- The hardness of water can be classified as soft, moderately hard, hard, or very hard based on the following table:

| Hardness of water (mg/L as CaCO3) | Classification |
| --------------------------------- | -------------- |
| < 75                             | Soft           |
| 75 - 150                         | Moderately hard|
| 150 - 300                        | Hard           |
| > 300                            | Very hard      |



# Determination of Alkalinity of Water Sample

- Alkalinity is a measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide ions in water.
- Alkalinity is important for aquatic life, water quality, and water treatment. It affects the pH, buffering capacity, and corrosion potential of water.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid, and measuring the volume of acid required to reach a specific pH endpoint.
- The most common endpoints for alkalinity titration are pH 4.5, pH 8.3, and pH 10.0. These correspond to the equivalence points of carbonic acid, bicarbonate, and carbonate, respectively.
- The total alkalinity of water is the sum of the alkalinity due to bicarbonate, carbonate, and hydroxide. It can be calculated from the volume of acid used to reach pH 4.5.
- The bicarbonate alkalinity of water is the alkalinity due to bicarbonate only. It can be calculated from the volume of acid used to reach pH 8.3.
- The carbonate alkalinity of water is the alkalinity due to carbonate and hydroxide. It can be calculated from the volume of acid used to reach pH 10.0.
- The hydroxide alkalinity of water is the alkalinity due to hydroxide only. It can be calculated from the difference between the carbonate alkalinity and the volume of acid used to reach pH 10.0.
- The phenolphthalein alkalinity of water is the alkalinity due to carbonate and hydroxide that can be detected by the color change of phenolphthalein indicator. It is equal to the volume of acid used to reach pH 8.3 minus the volume of acid used to reach pH 10.0.
- The methyl orange alkalinity of water is the alkalinity due to bicarbonate and carbonate that can be detected by the color change of methyl orange indicator. It is equal to the volume of acid used to reach pH 4.5 minus the volume of acid used to reach pH 8.3.



# Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte).
- pH is a measure of the acidity or alkalinity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H+].
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a pH meter to monitor the change in pH of the analyte solution as the titrant is added gradually.
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that is sensitive to hydrogen ions and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential. The potential difference between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution.
- A plot of the pH of the analyte solution versus the volume of the titrant added is called a pH curve or a titration curve. The shape of the curve depends on the nature and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration.
- The point where the titration curve changes slope sharply is called the endpoint or the equivalence point. It indicates that the analyte and the titrant have reacted stoichiometrically and the solution is neutralized.
- The volume of the titrant required to reach the endpoint can be used to calculate the concentration of the analyte using the following formula: C_aV_a = C_bV_b, where C_a and C_b are the concentrations of the analyte and the titrant, and V_a and V_b are the volumes of the analyte and the titrant.
- The accuracy and precision of the titrimetric method depend on the choice of the titrant, the calibration of the pH meter, the detection of the endpoint, and the quality of the glassware and pipettes.



# 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid

- Surface tension is a property of liquids that arises from the intermolecular forces between the molecules at the surface layer.
- Surface tension causes the liquid surface to behave like a stretched elastic membrane, and to minimize its area for a given volume.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the stalagmometer method.
- In this experiment, we will use the stalagmometer method to determine the surface tension of a given liquid.
- A stalagmometer is a device that consists of a glass tube with a bulb at one end and a fine nozzle at the other end.
- The tube is filled with the liquid to be tested, and then inverted over a graduated cylinder.
- The liquid drops out of the nozzle due to gravity, and the number of drops and the volume of liquid collected in the cylinder are recorded.
- The surface tension of the liquid is given by the formula:

$$\gamma = \frac{4\pi r^3 \rho g}{3V}$$

where $\gamma$ is the surface tension, $r$ is the radius of the nozzle, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity, and $V$ is the average volume of one drop.

- To perform the experiment, we need the following apparatus:

  - A stalagmometer
  - A graduated cylinder
  - A thermometer
  - A stopwatch
  - A balance
  - A liquid sample (such as water, alcohol, or glycerin)

- The procedure of the experiment is as follows:

  1. Clean and dry the stalagmometer and the graduated cylinder.
  2. Fill the stalagmometer with the liquid sample up to the mark on the bulb, and wipe off any excess liquid from the nozzle.
  3. Invert the stalagmometer over the graduated cylinder, and hold it vertically.
  4. Start the stopwatch and count the number of drops falling from the nozzle until the liquid level reaches the lower mark on the bulb.
  5. Stop the stopwatch and record the time taken, the number of drops, and the volume of liquid collected in the cylinder.
  6. Repeat the steps 2 to 5 for at least three trials, and calculate the average values of the time, the number of drops, and the volume of liquid.
  7. Measure the mass of the empty stalagmometer and the mass of the stalagmometer filled with the liquid sample, and calculate the mass of the liquid.
  8. Measure the temperature of the liquid sample, and look up the density of the liquid at that temperature from a reference table.
  9. Calculate the average volume of one drop by dividing the volume of liquid by the number of drops.
  10. Calculate the radius of the nozzle by using the formula:

  $$r = \sqrt{\frac{3V}{4\pi \rho g}}$$

  where $V$ is the average volume of one drop, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, and $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity.
  11. Calculate the surface tension of the liquid by using the formula:

  $$\gamma = \frac{4\pi r^3 \rho g}{3V}$$

  where $\gamma$ is the surface tension, $r$ is the radius of the nozzle, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity, and $V$ is the average volume of one drop.
  12. Report the result with the appropriate units and significant figures, and state the sources of error and uncertainty in the experiment.



# 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- Viscosity depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature and pressure of the fluid.
- A viscometer is a device that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The flow rate of the fluid is proportional to the pressure difference across the tube and inversely proportional to the viscosity of the fluid.
- The viscosity of a fluid can be calculated using the following formula:

$$\eta = \frac{\pi r^4 \Delta P}{8 Q L}$$

where $\eta$ is the viscosity, $r$ is the radius of the tube, $\Delta P$ is the pressure difference, $Q$ is the flow rate and $L$ is the length of the tube.

- To determine the viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer, the following steps are followed:

  - Fill the viscometer with the liquid and immerse it in a water bath at a constant temperature.
  - Measure the time taken for a fixed volume of the liquid to flow through the tube from one mark to another.
  - Repeat the experiment for different temperatures and record the data.
  - Calculate the viscosity of the liquid at each temperature using the formula.
  - Plot a graph of viscosity versus temperature and observe the trend.



# Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of iron(II) sulfate and ammonium sulfate, with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O.
- It is commonly used as a standard solution for titrating oxidizing agents, such as potassium permanganate or potassium dichromate, in redox titrations.
- The strength of a ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using an external indicator such as starch or potassium ferricyanide.
- The reaction between ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium permanganate is as follows:

    5(NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O + KMnO4 + 8H2SO4 -> 5Fe2(SO4)3 + 2MnSO4 + 10(NH4)2SO4 + K2SO4 + 48H2O

- The reaction is a self-indicating one, as the purple color of potassium permanganate disappears when all the ferrous ions are oxidized to ferric ions.
- However, near the end point, the color change is not very sharp, and an external indicator is needed to detect the exact end point.
- Starch is a common external indicator, as it forms a blue-black complex with iodine, which is produced by the reaction of excess potassium permanganate and potassium iodide:

    2KMnO4 + 10KI + 8H2SO4 -> 6K2SO4 + 2MnSO4 + 8H2O + 5I2

- The blue-black color of starch-iodine complex indicates the presence of excess potassium permanganate, and the end point is reached when the color disappears.
- Alternatively, potassium ferricyanide can be used as an external indicator, as it forms a blue precipitate of Turnbull's blue with ferrous ions, which are present before the end point:

    3Fe2+ + 2K3[Fe(CN)6] -> Fe3[Fe(CN)6]2 + 6K+

- The blue color of Turnbull's blue indicates the presence of ferrous ions, and the end point is reached when the color disappears.
- The procedure for determining the strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator is as follows:

    - Prepare a standard solution of potassium permanganate by dissolving a known mass of KMnO4 in distilled water and standardizing it against a primary standard, such as sodium oxalate or oxalic acid.
    - Pipette a known volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution into a conical flask and add some dilute sulfuric acid to provide the acidic medium for the reaction.
    - Add a few drops of starch or potassium ferricyanide solution as the external indicator to the flask.
    - Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution with the standard potassium permanganate solution from a burette, swirling the flask constantly, until the end point is reached, as indicated by the disappearance of the blue-black or blue color.
    - Record the final burette reading and calculate the volume of potassium permanganate used.
    - Repeat the titration at least two more times and take the average of the concordant results.
    - Use the balanced equation and the mole ratio to calculate the moles of ferrous ammonium sulfate reacted and the moles of potassium permanganate used.
    - Use the molar mass of ferrous ammonium sulfate to calculate the mass of ferrous ammonium sulfate reacted and the mass of ferrous ammonium sulfate in the pipetted volume.
    - Use the volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution to calculate the concentration or strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution in g/L or mol/L.



# Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a solution of an oxidizing agent, such as hydrogen peroxide, by titration.
- The internal indicator used in this experiment is diphenylamine sulfonic acid, which changes color from colorless to blue when oxidized by the excess of potassium dichromate.
- The procedure of the experiment is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of potassium dichromate by dissolving 0.25 g of the salt in 100 mL of distilled water.
  - Pipette 10 mL of the potassium dichromate solution into a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulfuric acid and a few drops of diphenylamine sulfonic acid indicator.
  - Titrate the solution with the given solution of hydrogen peroxide, using a burette, until the color changes from orange to green. Note the initial and final burette readings and calculate the volume of hydrogen peroxide used.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average volume of hydrogen peroxide used.
  - Calculate the strength of the hydrogen peroxide solution using the following formula:

    - Strength of hydrogen peroxide = (Volume of potassium dichromate x Normality of potassium dichromate x 5.68) / (Volume of hydrogen peroxide x 1000)

  - The normality of potassium dichromate can be calculated from its molecular weight and the number of electrons involved in the redox reaction, which is 6.

    - Normality of potassium dichromate = (Mass of potassium dichromate x 1000) / (Molecular weight of potassium dichromate x Volume of potassium dichromate x 6)

  - The molecular weight of potassium dichromate is 294.2 g/mol.



# Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder

- Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite (Ca(OCl)2), calcium chloride (CaCl2), and calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2).
- The available chlorine in bleaching powder is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated by the action of an acid on the hypochlorite ion (OCl-).
- The available chlorine can be determined by titrating a known mass of bleaching powder with a standard solution of sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3) using starch as an indicator.
- The reaction involved is:

OCl- + 2S2O3^2- -> Cl- + S4O6^2-

- The procedure is as follows:

  - Weigh accurately about 0.3 g of bleaching powder and transfer it to a 250 mL conical flask.
  - Add about 100 mL of distilled water and shake well to dissolve the bleaching powder.
  - Add 10 mL of 10% potassium iodide (KI) solution and 5 mL of 1 M sulfuric acid (H2SO4) and swirl the flask to mix the contents.
  - Titrate the liberated iodine (I2) with 0.1 M sodium thiosulfate solution until the yellow color of iodine fades.
  - Add a few drops of starch solution and continue the titration until the blue color of the starch-iodine complex disappears.
  - Note the final burette reading and repeat the titration for concordant results.
  - Calculate the percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder using the formula:

% available chlorine = (V x N x 35.5 x 100) / (W x 1000)

where V is the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used in mL, N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate solution, W is the mass of bleaching powder taken in g, and 35.5 is the equivalent mass of chlorine.



# Determination of chloride content in water sample

- Chloride is one of the major anions found in natural waters. It originates from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage.
- Chloride can affect the taste, corrosion, and biological activity of water. Therefore, it is important to monitor and control its concentration in drinking water, wastewater, and natural waters.
- One of the common methods for determining chloride content in water sample is the Mohr method, which is based on the precipitation of silver chloride by adding silver nitrate solution as a titrant.
- The Mohr method involves the following steps:

  - A known volume of water sample is transferred to a conical flask and a few drops of potassium chromate indicator are added. The indicator forms a yellow color in the solution.
  - A standard solution of silver nitrate is prepared and its concentration is determined by titrating against a standard solution of sodium chloride.
  - The silver nitrate solution is then titrated against the water sample until a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate appears, indicating the end point of the titration.
  - The volume of silver nitrate solution used is recorded and the chloride content in the water sample is calculated using the following formula:

    - Chloride content (mg/L) = (volume of AgNO3 used x normality of AgNO3 x 35.45) / volume of water sample

- The Mohr method has some limitations, such as interference from other anions that can precipitate with silver, such as bromide, iodide, sulfide, and cyanide. It also requires a neutral or slightly alkaline pH and a relatively low concentration of chloride (less than 250 mg/L).
- Other methods for determining chloride content in water sample include the Volhard method, the argentometric method, the mercurimetric method, the potentiometric method, and the ion chromatography method. Each method has its own advantages and disadvantages, depending on the accuracy, sensitivity, selectivity, and simplicity required.



# Preparation of Phenol Formaldehyde (PF) Resin

- Phenol formaldehyde resin is a type of thermosetting polymer that is obtained by the condensation reaction of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol and formaldehyde can react in different ratios to produce different types of PF resins, such as novolacs, resoles, and co-condensed resins.
- The reaction can be catalyzed by either acid or base, depending on the desired properties and applications of the resin.
- The general steps for the preparation of PF resin are:

  - Weigh the appropriate amount of phenol and formaldehyde, and mix them in a reaction vessel.
  - Add the catalyst, either acid or base, and heat the mixture to initiate the polymerization.
  - Monitor the reaction parameters, such as temperature, pH, viscosity, and water content, and adjust them as needed to control the degree of polymerization and the molecular weight distribution of the resin.
  - Stop the reaction when the desired properties are achieved, and isolate the resin by filtration, precipitation, or solvent extraction.
  - Dry and cure the resin, either by heating or by adding a hardener, to form a cross-linked network.

- The structure and properties of PF resin depend on the type and amount of catalyst, the molar ratio of phenol to formaldehyde, the reaction temperature and time, and the curing conditions.
- PF resin is widely used as an adhesive, a coating, a molding material, and a matrix for composite materials, due to its high strength, hardness, thermal stability, and chemical resistance.



# Preparation of Urea Formaldehyde (UF) Resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is synthesized by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst .
- The reaction can be carried out in one or more steps, depending on the desired properties and applications of the resin  .
- The following are some common methods of preparing UF resin:

## One-step method
- In this method, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 2-3:1 at pH 6-11.
- The mixture is heated to at least 80°C and then acidified to pH 0.5-3.5 with a mineral or organic acid.
- The acidification causes the formation of methylol groups and the subsequent polymerization of the resin.
- The resin is then cooled and neutralized with a base to adjust the pH to 7-9.
- The one-step method produces a resin with a low total extractable formaldehyde content and a high viscosity.

## Two-step method
- In this method, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1.5-2:1 at pH 8-9 .
- The mixture is heated to 60-80°C for 30-60 minutes to form monomethylolurea and dimethylolurea .
- The mixture is then cooled and stored in a refrigerator for two weeks to crystallize the monomethylolurea.
- The monomethylolurea is separated by filtration and washed with ethanol.
- The filtrate is then mixed with ammonia in a molar ratio of 0.5-1:1 and heated to 90-100°C for 30-60 minutes to form the resin .
- The ammonia acts as a catalyst and a modifier to improve the hydrolysis resistance and curing speed of the resin.
- The resin is then cooled and neutralized with an acid to adjust the pH to 7-9.
- The two-step method produces a resin with a low formaldehyde emission and a high solid content.

## Three-step method
- In this method, urea and formaldehyde are mixed in a molar ratio of 1:1.5-2 at pH 8-9.
- The mixture is heated to 60-80°C for 30-60 minutes to form monomethylolurea and dimethylolurea.
- The mixture is then cooled and acidified to pH 4-5 with a mineral or organic acid.
- The acidification causes the formation of methylene bridges and the subsequent polymerization of the resin.
- The resin is then heated to 90-100°C for 30-60 minutes to complete the polymerization.
- The resin is then cooled and neutralized with a base to adjust the pH to 7-9.
- The three-step method produces a resin with a high molecular weight and a high viscosity.



# Preparation of Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is an organic compound with the formula (CH2)4(COOH)2. It is a white crystalline powder that is used as a precursor for the production of nylon, polyurethanes, and other products of commercial interest.
- Adipic acid is produced from a mixture of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol called KA oil, the abbreviation of ketone-alcohol oil. The KA oil is oxidized with nitric acid to give adipic acid, via a multistep pathway .
- The oxidation of KA oil involves the following steps :
  - Formation of nitrosonium ion (NO+) by the reaction of nitric acid and sulfuric acid.
  - Nitrosation of cyclohexanone and cyclohexanol to form nitrosocyclohexane and nitrosocyclohexanol, respectively.
  - Dehydration of nitrosocyclohexanol to form cyclohexanone oxime.
  - Rearrangement of cyclohexanone oxime to form 6-nitrosohexanone.
  - Oxidation of 6-nitrosohexanone to form 6-nitrohexanoic acid.
  - Hydrolysis of 6-nitrohexanoic acid to form adipic acid and nitrous acid.
- The overall reaction can be written as :

  C6H10O + 2HNO3 -> C6H10O4 + 2NO + 2H2O

- The reaction is exothermic and requires careful control of temperature and pressure to avoid runaway reactions and explosions .
- The adipic acid is purified by crystallization from water and separated from the by-products such as nitrous oxide, nitric oxide, and water .



# 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a Solution

- Cell conductance is a measure of how well a solution can conduct electric current between two electrodes.
- Cell conductance depends on the concentration, type and mobility of the ions in the solution, as well as the geometry and material of the electrodes and the cell.
- Cell conductance can be determined experimentally by applying a known alternating voltage across the electrodes and measuring the resulting alternating current.
- Cell conductance can be calculated using the formula:

    `G = I / V`

    where G is the cell conductance, I is the current and V is the voltage.

- Cell conductance can also be expressed as the product of the cell constant and the specific conductance of the solution:

    `G = k * C`

    where k is the cell constant, which depends on the geometry and material of the electrodes and the cell, and C is the specific conductance of the solution, which depends on the concentration, type and mobility of the ions.

- The cell constant can be determined by measuring the cell conductance of a solution with a known specific conductance, such as a standard potassium chloride solution, and then using the formula:

    `k = G / C`

- The specific conductance of an unknown solution can then be determined by measuring its cell conductance and dividing it by the cell constant:

    `C = G / k`

- The specific conductance of a solution is related to its molar conductivity, which is the conductance of a solution containing one mole of electrolyte per unit volume, by the formula:

    `C = L * c`

    where L is the molar conductivity and c is the molar concentration of the electrolyte.

- The molar conductivity of a solution depends on the degree of dissociation of the electrolyte and the mobility of the ions. For strong electrolytes, such as salts, acids and bases, the molar conductivity decreases with increasing concentration, due to the increased interionic interactions and reduced mobility of the ions. For weak electrolytes, such as acetic acid and ammonia, the molar conductivity increases with increasing concentration, due to the increased degree of dissociation of the electrolyte and the increased number of ions.

- The molar conductivity of a solution at infinite dilution, where the interionic interactions are negligible and the degree of dissociation is complete, is denoted by L0 and can be calculated using the Kohlrausch's law of independent migration of ions, which states that the molar conductivity of a solution at infinite dilution is equal to the sum of the molar conductivities of the individual ions at infinite dilution, multiplied by their stoichiometric coefficients:

    `L0 = v+ * L0+ + v- * L0-`

    where v+ and v- are the stoichiometric coefficients of the cation and the anion, respectively, and L0+ and L0- are the molar conductivities of the cation and the anion at infinite dilution, respectively.

- The molar conductivities of the individual ions at infinite dilution can be determined experimentally by measuring the molar conductivities of different strong electrolytes containing the same ion at infinite dilution, and then using a system of linear equations to solve for the unknown values.

- The degree of dissociation of a weak electrolyte can be calculated using the formula:

    `α = L / L0`

    where α is the degree of dissociation, L is the molar conductivity of the weak electrolyte at a given concentration, and L0 is the molar conductivity of the weak electrolyte at infinite dilution.

- The dissociation constant of a weak electrolyte can be calculated using the formula:

    `K = α^2 * c / (1 - α)`

    where K is the dissociation constant, α is the degree of dissociation, and c is the molar concentration of the weak electrolyte.



# Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Esters are organic compounds that have the general formula RCOOR', where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.
- Esters can be hydrolyzed by water in the presence of an acid or a base to form carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- The hydrolysis of esters is a first-order reaction, which means that the rate of the reaction depends only on the concentration of the ester.
- The rate constant of the hydrolysis of esters can be determined by measuring the change in the concentration of the ester or the products over time, using methods such as titration, spectrophotometry, or conductometry.
- The rate constant of the hydrolysis of esters can be affected by factors such as temperature, pH, solvent, and catalysts.
- The rate constant of the hydrolysis of esters can be used to compare the reactivity of different esters, to study the mechanism of the reaction, and to estimate the shelf life of ester-containing products.



# Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Organic compounds are composed of carbon and hydrogen, and may also contain other elements such as oxygen, nitrogen, sulfur, phosphorus, halogens, etc.
- Element detection is the process of determining the presence or absence of a particular element in an organic compound.
- Identification of functional groups is the process of determining the type and nature of the specific groups of atoms that are responsible for the characteristic chemical reactions of an organic compound.
- Some of the common methods for element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds are:

  - **Lassaigne's test**: This is a general test for the detection of nitrogen, sulfur and halogens in an organic compound. It involves fusing the organic compound with sodium metal in a porcelain crucible, and then dissolving the fused mass in distilled water. The resulting solution is called sodium extract, and it is used for various tests to detect the elements.
  - **Test for nitrogen**: The sodium extract is boiled with iron(II) sulfate and concentrated sulfuric acid. If nitrogen is present, a brown ring is formed at the junction of the two layers, due to the formation of iron(III) nitrosyl complex.
  - **Test for sulfur**: The sodium extract is acidified with acetic acid and lead acetate is added. If sulfur is present, a black precipitate of lead sulfide is formed.
  - **Test for halogens**: The sodium extract is acidified with nitric acid and silver nitrate is added. If halogens are present, a precipitate of silver halide is formed, which is white for chloride, pale yellow for bromide and yellow for iodide. The precipitates can be further distinguished by their solubility in ammonia solution.
  - **Test for oxygen**: There is no specific test for oxygen, but its presence can be inferred from the presence of other elements and functional groups that contain oxygen, such as alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, esters, ethers, etc.
  - **Test for phosphorus**: The organic compound is heated with an oxidizing agent, such as nitric acid or sodium peroxide, and the resulting solution is treated with ammonium molybdate. If phosphorus is present, a yellow precipitate of ammonium phosphomolybdate is formed.
  - **Test for functional groups**: There are various tests for different functional groups, based on their characteristic reactions with specific reagents. Some of the common tests are:

    - **Test for alcohols**: Alcohols are organic compounds that contain a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a carbon atom. Some of the tests for alcohols are:

      - **Lucas test**: This test is used to distinguish between primary, secondary and tertiary alcohols. The alcohol is treated with Lucas reagent, which is a mixture of zinc chloride and concentrated hydrochloric acid. If a tertiary alcohol is present, a turbidity or a cloudy layer is formed immediately, due to the formation of a water-insoluble alkyl chloride. If a secondary alcohol is present, a turbidity is formed after 5 to 10 minutes. If a primary alcohol is present, no turbidity is formed even after a long time.
      - **Victor Meyer's test**: This test is used to distinguish between aliphatic and aromatic alcohols. The alcohol is heated with iodine and sodium hydroxide, and the resulting vapors are passed through a condenser and collected in a test tube containing nitric acid and silver nitrate. If an aliphatic alcohol is present, a red or brown color is formed, due to the formation of iodoform. If an aromatic alcohol is present, no color change is observed.
      - **Esterification test**: This test is used to confirm the presence of an alcohol. The alcohol is heated with an acid, such as acetic acid or sulfuric acid, and the resulting mixture is cooled and poured into water. If an alcohol is present, a fruity smell is observed, due to the formation of an ester.

    - **Test for aldehydes and ketones**: Aldehydes and ketones are organic compounds that contain a carbonyl group (>C=O) attached to a carbon atom. Some of the tests for aldehydes and ketones are:

      - **Fehling's test**: This test is used to distinguish between aldehydes and ketones. The aldehyde or ketone is treated with Fehling's solution, which is a mixture of copper(II) sulfate, sodium hydroxide and potassium sodium tartrate. If an aldehyde is present, a red precipitate of copper

# Course Outcomes:

- A course outcome is a statement that describes what a student should be able to do or demonstrate after completing a course.
- Course outcomes are usually derived from the course objectives, which are the broad goals of the course.
- Course outcomes are more specific, measurable, and observable than course objectives.
- Course outcomes should align with the course content, activities, and assessments, as well as the program outcomes and the institutional mission and vision.
- Course outcomes should be written using action verbs that indicate the level of cognitive, affective, or psychomotor skills expected from the students.
- Course outcomes should be clear, concise, and consistent, and should avoid vague or ambiguous terms.
- Course outcomes should be communicated to the students at the beginning of the course, and should be used as a guide for their learning and self-evaluation.
- Course outcomes should be reviewed and revised periodically to ensure their relevance, validity, and effectiveness.



# Upon completion of the course the student should be able to:

- Identify and explain the main concepts, theories and methods of the discipline.
- Apply the relevant knowledge and skills to analyze and solve problems in the domain of the course.
- Communicate effectively and professionally using appropriate terminology and formats.
- Demonstrate critical thinking and creativity in approaching complex and novel situations.
- Collaborate with others and work independently as required by the course objectives and expectations.
- Reflect on their own learning process and outcomes and seek feedback for improvement.
- Recognize and adhere to the ethical and social responsibilities of the discipline and the profession.



# Course Outcomes Bloom's

- Course outcomes are statements that describe what students should be able to do or demonstrate after completing a course.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a framework for classifying educational objectives into six levels of cognitive complexity: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create.
- Course outcomes can be aligned with Bloom's taxonomy to ensure that they cover a range of cognitive skills and learning domains.
- Some examples of course outcomes and their corresponding Bloom's levels are:

  - Remember: Recall the basic concepts and principles of accounting.
  - Understand: Explain the difference between cash and accrual accounting methods.
  - Apply: Prepare journal entries and financial statements using the accounting equation.
  - Analyze: Interpret the financial ratios and indicators of a company's performance and liquidity.
  - Evaluate: Compare and contrast the advantages and disadvantages of different accounting standards and policies.
  - Create: Design and propose a budget plan for a business project.

- Course outcomes should be specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound (SMART) to ensure that they are clear, realistic, and assessable.
- Course outcomes should also be aligned with the course content, activities, and assessments to ensure that they are consistent and coherent.



# Level

- A level is a tool or device that is used to measure or indicate whether a surface is horizontal (level) or vertical (plumb).
- A level consists of a horizontal tube or vial that contains a liquid (usually alcohol or water) and an air bubble. The position of the bubble indicates the alignment of the surface relative to the earth's gravity.
- A level can be used for various purposes, such as construction, carpentry, engineering, surveying, landscaping, photography, etc.
- There are different types of levels, such as spirit levels, laser levels, digital levels, water levels, etc. Each type has its own advantages and disadvantages depending on the application and accuracy required.
- A level can be calibrated by placing it on a flat surface and adjusting it until the bubble is centered in the vial. Then, the level is rotated 180 degrees and placed on the same surface. If the bubble is still centered, the level is accurate. If not, the level needs to be adjusted or replaced.



# CO-1 Get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments. K3

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or identify the chemical, physical, or biological properties of a sample or a substance.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on the principle or technique of analysis, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, mass spectrometry, electrochemistry, thermal analysis, microscopy, etc.
- Analytical instruments have various applications in different fields, such as pharmaceutical, food, environmental, clinical, forensic, life science, chemical, etc.
- Analytical instruments can help in determining the composition, structure, purity, quality, quantity, or function of a sample or a substance.
- Analytical instruments can also help in solving problems, testing hypotheses, developing new methods, or validating existing methods.

Some examples of analytical instruments and their uses are:

- Refractometer: A device that measures the refractive index of a liquid or a solid, which is related to its concentration, density, or purity. Refractometers are used in food, beverage, pharmaceutical, chemical, and petrochemical industries.
- Spectrophotometer: A device that measures the amount of light absorbed or transmitted by a sample at different wavelengths, which is related to its color, concentration, or chemical structure. Spectrophotometers are used in biochemistry, molecular biology, environmental science, and quality control.
- Electrochemical instrument: A device that measures the electrical properties of a sample, such as voltage, current, resistance, or impedance, which are related to its chemical reactions, oxidation, or corrosion. Electrochemical instruments are used in electrochemistry, battery testing, corrosion monitoring, and biosensors.
- Calorimeter: A device that measures the amount of heat released or absorbed by a sample during a chemical or physical process, which is related to its energy, enthalpy, or thermodynamics. Calorimeters are used in calorimetry, combustion analysis, and material science.
- Automatic density meter: A device that measures the density of a liquid or a gas, which is related to its mass, volume, or specific gravity. Automatic density meters are used in petroleum, chemical, pharmaceutical, and beverage industries.
- Conductivity meter: A device that measures the electrical conductivity of a solution, which is related to its ion concentration, salinity, or purity. Conductivity meters are used in water quality, environmental monitoring, and chemical analysis.
- Colony counter: A device that counts the number of bacterial colonies on a culture plate, which is related to their growth, viability, or enumeration. Colony counters are used in microbiology, food safety, and clinical diagnostics.
- Automatic titrator: A device that performs a titration, which is a method of finding the concentration of an unknown solution by adding a known solution of another substance until a color change or an endpoint is reached. Automatic titrators are used in acid-base, redox, complexometric, or precipitation titrations.
- Fiberscope: A device that consists of a flexible optical fiber bundle that can transmit light and images from a remote location, which is related to its inspection, observation, or diagnosis. Fiberscopes are used in endoscopy, industrial inspection, and security.
- Demagnetizer: A device that removes or reduces the magnetic field of a material, which is related to its magnetization, alignment, or performance. Demagnetizers are used in magnetic recording, magnetic resonance imaging, and magnetic therapy.



# CO2: Molecular and System Properties

- Carbon dioxide (CO2) is a linear molecule with a bond length of 116.3 pm between the carbon and the oxygen atoms and a bond angle of 180 degrees.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero. It has four vibrational modes: symmetric stretching, asymmetric stretching, and two degenerate bending modes.
- CO2 is a gas at standard temperature and pressure, with a density of 1.977 kg/m3 and a molar mass of 44.0095 g/mol .
- CO2 can be dissolved in water and other solvents, forming weak carbonic acid (H2CO3) and bicarbonate (HCO3-) and carbonate (CO3 2-) ions. The solubility of CO2 in water decreases with increasing temperature and increases with increasing pressure.
- CO2 can also react with some bases, such as monoethanolamine (MEA), to form carbamate salts. These reactions are reversible and depend on the temperature, pressure, and concentration of the reactants .
- Surface tension is the energy, or work, required to increase the surface area of a liquid due to intermolecular forces. Surface tension is measured in units of N/m or J/m2.
- The surface tension of pure water at 20 °C is 72.8 mN/m. The surface tension of water decreases with increasing temperature and increases with increasing salinity.
- The surface tension of CO2-loaded aqueous solutions depends on the concentration of CO2, the type and concentration of the solvent, and the temperature. For example, the surface tension of aqueous MEA solutions decreases with increasing CO2 loading and increasing temperature, and increases with increasing MEA concentration .
- The surface tension of CO2-loaded aqueous solutions can be measured by various methods, such as the pendant drop method, the Wilhelmy plate method, the maximum bubble pressure method, or the capillary rise method .



# Viscosity

- Viscosity is the property of a fluid that measures its resistance to flow.
- Viscosity depends on the internal friction between the molecules of the fluid, which is affected by factors such as temperature, pressure, and composition.
- Viscosity can be expressed by the formula: 

    `η = F / (A * dv/dy)`

    where η is the viscosity, F is the force applied, A is the area of contact, and dv/dy is the velocity gradient of the fluid.
- Viscosity has units of pascal-seconds (Pa·s) in the SI system, or poise (P) in the CGS system. One poise is equal to 0.1 Pa·s.
- Viscosity can be classified into two types: dynamic viscosity and kinematic viscosity. Dynamic viscosity is the ratio of shear stress to shear rate, while kinematic viscosity is the ratio of dynamic viscosity to density.
- Viscosity can be measured by various methods, such as capillary viscometers, rotational viscometers, falling ball viscometers, and rheometers.
- Viscosity is an important parameter in many applications, such as lubrication, fluid dynamics, transport phenomena, and polymer science.

- K3 is a type of visa that allows a foreign spouse of a U.S. citizen to enter and reside in the United States while waiting for the approval of their immigrant visa petition.
- K3 visa is a nonimmigrant visa that is valid for two years and can be renewed if the immigrant visa petition is still pending.
- K3 visa holders are eligible to apply for work authorization and travel permission while in the United States.
- K3 visa holders are also eligible to adjust their status to permanent residents once their immigrant visa petition is approved and a visa number is available.
- K3 visa is one of the options for spouses of U.S. citizens who wish to reunite with their partners in the United States. The other option is to apply for an immigrant visa directly at a U.S. consulate abroad, which may take longer depending on the processing time and availability of visa numbers.



# CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness and alkalinity are two important parameters of water quality that affect its suitability for various purposes.
- Hardness is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water, which can form insoluble deposits or scale on pipes, boilers, and other equipment. Hardness can also affect the taste, lather formation, and detergent efficiency of water.
- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids, which is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, and other metals. Alkalinity can also affect the pH, corrosion, and buffering capacity of water.
- The hardness and alkalinity of water can be measured by titration methods using standard solutions of known concentration and indicators that change color at specific pH values.
- The common methods for measuring hardness and alkalinity are:

  - EDTA titration method for total hardness and calcium hardness. EDTA is a chelating agent that forms a complex with calcium and magnesium ions, and the end point is detected by a metal indicator such as Eriochrome Black T or Calcon, which changes color from red to blue when all the metal ions are complexed.
  - Soap titration method for total hardness. Soap is a sodium salt of a fatty acid that forms a precipitate with calcium and magnesium ions, and the end point is detected by the formation of a stable lather or foam on the surface of the water sample.
  - Phenolphthalein and methyl orange titration methods for total alkalinity and bicarbonate alkalinity. Phenolphthalein is an indicator that changes color from colorless to pink at pH 8.3, and methyl orange is an indicator that changes color from yellow to red at pH 4.3. The total alkalinity is the amount of acid required to lower the pH of the water sample to 4.3, and the bicarbonate alkalinity is the amount of acid required to lower the pH of the water sample to 8.3. The difference between the total alkalinity and the bicarbonate alkalinity is the carbonate alkalinity, which is due to the presence of carbonate ions in water.
  - Phenolphthalein titration method for hydroxide alkalinity. Phenolphthalein is an indicator that changes color from colorless to pink at pH 8.3, and the hydroxide alkalinity is the amount of acid required to lower the pH of the water sample to 8.3. The hydroxide alkalinity is due to the presence of hydroxide ions in water, which are usually formed by the dissociation of calcium and magnesium hydroxides.



# CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

- Phenol is an aromatic compound with a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring.
- Phenol is also known as carbolic acid or hydroxybenzene.
- Phenol has a pungent smell and is corrosive to the skin and eyes.
- Phenol is used as an antiseptic, disinfectant, and precursor for many industrial chemicals and drugs.

## Preparation of phenol

- Phenol can be prepared from various sources, such as coal tar, benzene, cumene, and chlorobenzene.
- Some of the common methods of preparation of phenol are:

### From coal tar

- Coal tar is a by-product of the destructive distillation of coal.
- Coal tar contains about 1% of phenol along with other aromatic compounds.
- Phenol can be extracted from coal tar by fractional distillation, followed by washing with sodium hydroxide solution to remove acidic impurities.
- The phenol can then be purified by crystallization or distillation.

### From benzene

- Benzene can be converted to phenol by electrophilic substitution with nitric acid, followed by reduction with tin and hydrochloric acid.
- The reaction steps are:

  - Benzene + Nitric acid -> Nitrobenzene + Water
  - Nitrobenzene + Tin + Hydrochloric acid -> Phenol + Ammonium chloride

- Alternatively, benzene can be converted to phenol by sulfonation with concentrated sulfuric acid, followed by hydrolysis with steam.
- The reaction steps are:

  - Benzene + Sulfuric acid -> Benzenesulfonic acid + Water
  - Benzenesulfonic acid + Steam -> Phenol + Sulfur dioxide

### From cumene

- Cumene is an alkylbenzene with an isopropyl group attached to a benzene ring.
- Cumene can be oxidized to cumene hydroperoxide, which can be cleaved to phenol and acetone by treatment with dilute acid.
- This method is known as the cumene process and is widely used in the industrial production of phenol and acetone.
- The reaction steps are:

  - Cumene + Oxygen -> Cumene hydroperoxide
  - Cumene hydroperoxide + Dilute acid -> Phenol + Acetone

### From chlorobenzene

- Chlorobenzene is an aromatic compound with a chlorine atom attached to a benzene ring.
- Chlorobenzene can be hydrolyzed to phenol by heating with sodium hydroxide solution under high pressure.
- This method is known as the Dow process and is used to produce phenol from waste chlorobenzene.
- The reaction steps are:

  - Chlorobenzene + Sodium hydroxide -> Sodium phenoxide + Sodium chloride
  - Sodium phenoxide + Water -> Phenol + Sodium hydroxide



# Formaldehyde and Urea Formaldehyde Resin

- Formaldehyde is a colorless, flammable, strong-smelling chemical that is used in building materials and to produce many household products.
- Formaldehyde can cause irritation of the eyes, nose, and throat, and can increase the risk of some cancers.
- Urea formaldehyde (UF) is a nontransparent thermosetting resin or polymer that is produced from urea and formaldehyde.
- UF is used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF can release formaldehyde gas into the air, which can cause health problems for people who are exposed to it.
- UF can also show the appearance of crystal domains, which can affect its morphology and crystallinity .
- The crystallization of UF can be influenced by the synthesis conditions, such as pH, temperature, and molar ratio of urea to formaldehyde .
- The crystallization of UF can also be modified by adding certain substances, such as melamine, polyvinyl alcohol, and adipic acid dihydrazide, which can reduce the free formaldehyde content and improve the performance of UF.

# Adipic Acid

- Adipic acid is a white, crystalline organic acid that is mainly used to produce nylon and polyurethane.
- Adipic acid is also used as a food additive, a plasticizer, a lubricant, and a corrosion inhibitor.
- Adipic acid can be synthesized from various raw materials, such as cyclohexane, benzene, glucose, and bio-based sources.
- Adipic acid can also be produced by oxidation of cyclohexanol or cyclohexanone with nitric acid or hydrogen peroxide.
- Adipic acid can cause irritation of the skin, eyes, and respiratory tract, and can also affect the central nervous system and the kidneys.

# Paracetamol

- Paracetamol, also known as acetaminophen, is a common over-the-counter pain reliever and fever reducer.
- Paracetamol is used to treat mild to moderate pain, such as headache, toothache, muscle ache, menstrual cramps, and arthritis.
- Paracetamol is also used to reduce fever and to relieve the symptoms of cold and flu.
- Paracetamol can be taken orally, rectally, or intravenously, depending on the dosage form and the condition being treated.
- Paracetamol can cause side effects, such as nausea, vomiting, rash, and allergic reactions.
- Paracetamol can also cause liver damage if taken in large doses or with alcohol or other drugs that affect the liver.
- Paracetamol can be synthesized from phenol or p-aminophenol with acetic anhydride or acetyl chloride.



# K3

- K3 is a type of surface in mathematics that is a complex, compact, smooth, algebraic variety of dimension two with trivial canonical bundle.
- K3 surfaces are named after the mathematicians Kummer, Kähler, and Kodaira, who studied them in the 19th and 20th centuries.
- K3 surfaces have many remarkable properties and applications in geometry, topology, number theory, and physics.
- Some examples of K3 surfaces are:

  - The quartic surface in projective 3-space defined by a homogeneous polynomial of degree four.
  - The double cover of the projective plane branched along a smooth sextic curve.
  - The resolution of singularities of a cubic surface in projective 3-space with four ordinary double points.
  - The moduli space of elliptic curves with level 2 structure.

- Some properties of K3 surfaces are:

  - They have Euler characteristic 24 and Picard number at most 20.
  - They have 20-dimensional Hodge structures on their second cohomology groups, which are isomorphic to the lattice of the Leech root system.
  - They have a unique holomorphic 2-form up to scalar multiplication, which induces a symplectic structure on their smooth points.
  - They are Ricci-flat and admit hyperkähler metrics, which are special cases of Calabi-Yau metrics.
  - They are simply connected and have trivial fundamental group.
  - They are rigid, meaning that they have no non-trivial deformations of their complex structures.



# CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction K3

- The rate constant of a reaction is a proportionality factor that relates the reaction rate to the concentrations of the reactants.
- The rate constant depends on the temperature and the activation energy of the reaction.
- The rate constant can have different units depending on the order of the reaction.
- For a termolecular reaction, where three molecules collide simultaneously, the reaction rate is described by r=k3[A][B][C], where k3 is a termolecular rate constant.
- To estimate the rate constant of a termolecular reaction, one can use the Arrhenius equation, which relates the rate constant to the temperature and the activation energy of the reaction.
- The Arrhenius equation is k=Ae^(-Ea/RT), where k is the rate constant, A is the pre-exponential factor, e is the base of the natural logarithm, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature.
- If the activation energy and the pre-exponential factor of the termolecular reaction are known, one can estimate the rate constant k3 at a given temperature by plugging in the values into the Arrhenius equation.
- Alternatively, if the rate constant k3 is known at two different temperatures, one can use the two-point form of the Arrhenius equation to estimate the activation energy and the pre-exponential factor of the termolecular reaction.
- The two-point form of the Arrhenius equation is ln(k2/k1)=(-Ea/R)(1/T2-1/T1), where k1 and k2 are the rate constants at temperatures T1 and T2, respectively.
- Once the activation energy and the pre-exponential factor are obtained, one can estimate the rate constant k3 at any other temperature using the Arrhenius equation.
- In some cases, the termolecular reaction may take place in multiple steps, each with its own rate constant and activation energy .
- In such cases, one has to identify the rate-determining step, which is the slowest step that controls the overall rate of the reaction .
- The rate constant of the rate-determining step is equal to the overall rate constant of the termolecular reaction .
- To estimate the rate constant of the rate-determining step, one can use the same methods as described above for a single-step termolecular reaction .
- However, one has to be careful to use the correct activation energy and pre-exponential factor for the rate-determining step, which may not be the same as the ones for the other steps .

# Nuclear Fusion

Nuclear fusion is the process of combining two or more atomic nuclei to form a heavier nucleus, releasing energy in the process. Nuclear fusion is the source of energy for the Sun and other stars.

Some points to remember about nuclear fusion are:

- Nuclear fusion requires high temperatures and pressures to overcome the electrostatic repulsion between positively charged nuclei. The temperature needed for fusion is around 100 million degrees Celsius.
- Nuclear fusion can produce more energy per unit mass than nuclear fission, which is the process of splitting a heavy nucleus into lighter ones. For example, the fusion of four hydrogen atoms into one helium atom releases about 17.6 MeV of energy, while the fission of one uranium atom releases about 200 MeV of energy.
- Nuclear fusion can also produce less radioactive waste than nuclear fission, as the fusion products are generally stable or have short half-lives. However, nuclear fusion can also generate high-energy neutrons that can damage the materials and components of the fusion reactor.
- Nuclear fusion is not yet a viable source of energy for human use, as the current fusion experiments consume more energy than they produce. The main challenges for achieving controlled and sustained fusion are finding suitable fuels, creating and confining the plasma, and extracting the energy efficiently.
- Some of the potential fuels for nuclear fusion are deuterium and tritium, which are isotopes of hydrogen. Deuterium is abundant in seawater, while tritium can be produced from lithium. The most common fusion reaction is the deuterium-tritium (D-T) reaction, which has a high cross-section and a low ignition temperature.
- Some of the methods for creating and confining the plasma are magnetic confinement and inertial confinement. Magnetic confinement uses strong magnetic fields to contain the plasma in a torus-shaped device called a tokamak. Inertial confinement uses powerful lasers or ion beams to compress and heat a small pellet of fuel to initiate fusion.
- Some of the ways for extracting the energy from fusion are direct conversion and thermal conversion. Direct conversion converts the kinetic energy of the charged fusion products into electricity using electric fields. Thermal conversion uses the heat generated by the fusion reactions and the neutrons to drive a conventional steam turbine.
