# Nuclear Fusion

Nuclear fusion is the process of combining two or more atomic nuclei into a single, heavier nucleus, releasing a large amount of energy in the process. Nuclear fusion is the source of energy for the Sun and other stars.

Some of the main points to know about nuclear fusion are:

- Nuclear fusion requires high temperatures and pressures to overcome the electrostatic repulsion between positively charged nuclei. The conditions for fusion are achieved in the cores of stars, where the temperature is about 15 million degrees Celsius and the pressure is about 200 billion atmospheres.
- The most common fusion reaction in stars is the proton-proton chain, which converts four hydrogen nuclei (protons) into one helium nucleus (alpha particle), releasing two positrons, two neutrinos, and gamma rays. This reaction converts about 0.7% of the mass of the hydrogen into energy, according to Einstein's famous equation E = mc^2.
- Another fusion reaction that occurs in stars is the carbon-nitrogen-oxygen (CNO) cycle, which uses carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen as catalysts to fuse hydrogen into helium. This reaction is more efficient than the proton-proton chain, but requires higher temperatures and densities to operate. The CNO cycle dominates in stars that are more massive than the Sun.
- Nuclear fusion can also be achieved artificially in devices called fusion reactors, which aim to harness the energy of fusion for power generation or other purposes. Fusion reactors use various methods to confine and heat the plasma, which is a state of matter where the nuclei and electrons are separated. Some of the methods are magnetic confinement, inertial confinement, and electrostatic confinement.
- The most widely studied fusion reaction for reactors is the deuterium-tritium (D-T) reaction, which fuses a deuterium nucleus (one proton and one neutron) with a tritium nucleus (one proton and two neutrons) into a helium nucleus and a neutron, releasing a lot of energy. Deuterium is abundant in seawater, and tritium can be produced from lithium, making the D-T reaction a potential source of clean and virtually unlimited energy.
- However, achieving controlled and sustained fusion in reactors is very challenging, as it requires high temperatures (about 100 million degrees Celsius), high pressures, and high confinement times. The current record for fusion power output is held by the Joint European Torus (JET) in the UK, which produced 16 megawatts of fusion power in 1997, but consumed 24 megawatts of input power. The international project ITER, under construction in France, aims to achieve 500 megawatts of fusion power with 50 megawatts of input power by 2035.



# Page 32 of 40

- This page covers the topic of **linear regression**, which is a statistical method for modeling the relationship between a dependent variable and one or more independent variables.
- Linear regression assumes that the dependent variable can be expressed as a linear combination of the independent variables, plus some random error term.
- Linear regression can be used for various purposes, such as:
  - Exploring the nature and strength of the relationship between variables
  - Testing hypotheses about the effects of variables on the outcome
  - Predicting the value of the dependent variable for new observations of the independent variables
  - Evaluating the fit and accuracy of the model
- Linear regression can be classified into two types, depending on the number of independent variables:
  - Simple linear regression: only one independent variable
  - Multiple linear regression: two or more independent variables
- The general form of a linear regression model is:

  - y = β0 + β1x1 + β2x2 + ... + βkxk + ε
  - where y is the dependent variable, x1, x2, ... , xk are the independent variables, β0, β1, ... , βk are the coefficients or parameters of the model, and ε is the error term.
- The coefficients of the model represent the slope or effect of each independent variable on the dependent variable, while the error term captures the variation in the dependent variable that is not explained by the model.
- The goal of linear regression is to estimate the coefficients of the model that best fit the observed data, and to assess the quality and significance of the model.
- There are different methods for estimating the coefficients of a linear regression model, such as:
  - Ordinary least squares (OLS): minimizes the sum of squared errors between the observed and predicted values of the dependent variable
  - Maximum likelihood estimation (MLE): maximizes the probability of observing the data given the model
  - Bayesian inference: incorporates prior knowledge and uncertainty about the coefficients into the estimation process
- There are different criteria for evaluating the fit and accuracy of a linear regression model, such as:
  - R-squared: measures the proportion of variance in the dependent variable that is explained by the model
  - Adjusted R-squared: adjusts the R-squared for the number of independent variables in the model
  - Root mean squared error (RMSE): measures the average deviation of the predicted values from the observed values
  - F-test: tests the overall significance of the model by comparing it to a null model with no independent variables
  - T-test: tests the significance of each coefficient by comparing it to zero
  - Confidence intervals: provide a range of plausible values for each coefficient based on the estimation error
  - Residual analysis: examines the distribution and patterns of the error term to check for violations of the assumptions of linear regression
- Some of the assumptions of linear regression are:
  - Linearity: the relationship between the dependent variable and the independent variables is linear
  - Independence: the observations are independent of each other
  - Homoscedasticity: the variance of the error term is constant across the values of the independent variables
  - Normality: the error term follows a normal distribution
  - No multicollinearity: the independent variables are not highly correlated with each other
- If some of the assumptions of linear regression are violated, the model may produce biased, inconsistent, or inefficient estimates of the coefficients, and the inference may be invalid or unreliable.
- There are different ways to address the violations of the assumptions of linear regression, such as:
  - Transforming the variables to improve the linearity of the relationship
  - Adding or removing variables to reduce the multicollinearity
  - Using robust or weighted methods to account for the heteroscedasticity or non-normality of the error term
  - Using generalized linear models to handle non-linear or non-continuous dependent variables

# Nuclear Fusion

Nuclear fusion is the process of combining two or more atomic nuclei into a single, heavier nucleus, releasing a large amount of energy in the process. Nuclear fusion is the source of energy for the Sun and other stars.

Some of the main points to know about nuclear fusion are:

- Nuclear fusion requires high temperatures and pressures to overcome the electrostatic repulsion between positively charged nuclei. The conditions for fusion are achieved in the cores of stars, where the temperature is about 15 million degrees Celsius and the pressure is about 200 billion atmospheres.
- The most common fusion reaction in stars is the proton-proton chain, which converts four hydrogen nuclei (protons) into one helium nucleus (alpha particle), releasing two positrons, two neutrinos, and gamma rays. This reaction converts about 0.7% of the mass of the hydrogen into energy, according to Einstein's famous equation E = mc^2.
- Another fusion reaction that occurs in stars is the carbon-nitrogen-oxygen (CNO) cycle, which uses carbon, nitrogen, and oxygen as catalysts to fuse hydrogen into helium. This reaction is more efficient than the proton-proton chain, but requires higher temperatures and densities to operate. The CNO cycle dominates in stars that are more massive than the Sun.
- Nuclear fusion can also occur artificially in devices called fusion reactors, which aim to harness the energy of fusion for human use. Fusion reactors use different types of fuel, such as deuterium and tritium, which are isotopes of hydrogen with one and two neutrons, respectively. These isotopes are easier to fuse than ordinary hydrogen, but still require very high temperatures and pressures to initiate and sustain the fusion reaction.
- One of the main challenges of fusion reactors is to confine the hot plasma, which is a state of matter where the atoms are ionized and the electrons and nuclei are separated. The plasma can reach temperatures of hundreds of millions of degrees Celsius, which can damage any material that comes in contact with it. Therefore, fusion reactors use magnetic fields to keep the plasma away from the walls of the reactor. This is called magnetic confinement.
- Another challenge of fusion reactors is to achieve a net energy gain, which means that the energy output of the fusion reaction is greater than the energy input to heat and confine the plasma. This is measured by a parameter called the Q factor, which is the ratio of the fusion power to the input power. A Q factor of 1 means that the fusion reactor is breaking even, while a Q factor greater than 1 means that the fusion reactor is producing more energy than it consumes. The highest Q factor achieved so far in a fusion reactor is about 0.67, by the Joint European Torus (JET) in 1997.
- The most promising design for a fusion reactor is the tokamak, which is a doughnut-shaped device that uses a strong toroidal (ring-shaped) magnetic field to confine the plasma. The tokamak was invented by Soviet physicists in the 1950s and has been developed by many countries since then. The largest and most advanced tokamak in the world is the International Thermonuclear Experimental Reactor (ITER), which is being built in France by a consortium of 35 nations. ITER aims to achieve a Q factor of at least 10, which would be a major breakthrough for fusion energy. ITER is expected to start operations in 2025 and reach full power by 2035.



# Engineering Mathematics Lab

Engineering mathematics is the application of mathematical methods and techniques to solve engineering problems. Engineering mathematics lab is a practical course that complements the theoretical aspects of engineering mathematics and helps students to develop their mathematical skills and problem-solving abilities.

The syllabus of engineering mathematics lab may vary depending on the institution and the branch of engineering. However, some common topics that are covered in engineering mathematics lab are:

- Numerical methods: These are methods to find approximate solutions of mathematical problems using algorithms and computer programs. Some examples of numerical methods are root-finding, interpolation, differentiation, integration, linear and nonlinear equations, ordinary and partial differential equations, etc.
- Linear algebra: This is the study of vector spaces, matrices, determinants, linear transformations, eigenvalues, eigenvectors, etc. Linear algebra is useful for solving systems of linear equations, performing matrix operations, analyzing linear systems, etc.
- Probability and statistics: These are branches of mathematics that deal with uncertainty, randomness, data analysis, inference, etc. Probability and statistics are useful for modeling and analyzing phenomena that involve chance, variability, sampling, testing, etc.
- Complex analysis: This is the study of complex numbers, functions of complex variables, analytic functions, contour integration, residues, etc. Complex analysis is useful for solving problems involving harmonic functions, potential theory, Fourier series, Laplace transforms, etc.
- Fourier analysis: This is the study of periodic functions, Fourier series, Fourier transforms, discrete Fourier transforms, etc. Fourier analysis is useful for analyzing signals, waves, heat conduction, etc.
- Laplace transforms: These are integral transforms that convert differential equations into algebraic equations. Laplace transforms are useful for solving problems involving initial value problems, transfer functions, stability, etc.

The objectives of engineering mathematics lab are:

- To enhance the understanding of the theoretical concepts of engineering mathematics by applying them to practical problems.
- To develop the computational skills and programming abilities of the students using various software tools and platforms.
- To expose the students to the applications of engineering mathematics in various fields of engineering and science.
- To foster the analytical and critical thinking skills of the students and to encourage them to explore new ideas and methods.

The outcomes of engineering mathematics lab are:

- The students will be able to apply the numerical methods to find approximate solutions of various mathematical problems using computer programs.
- The students will be able to perform the matrix operations and solve the systems of linear equations using linear algebra techniques and software tools.
- The students will be able to analyze and interpret the data using probability and statistics methods and software tools.
- The students will be able to solve the problems involving complex variables and functions using complex analysis techniques and software tools.
- The students will be able to analyze the periodic functions and signals using Fourier analysis techniques and software tools.
- The students will be able to solve the differential equations using Laplace transforms techniques and software tools.

The evaluation of engineering mathematics lab may be based on the following criteria:

- Attendance and participation in the lab sessions.
- Performance in the lab assignments and quizzes.
- Submission of the lab reports and projects.
- Performance in the lab exams and viva-voce.



## Course Objectives:

- To provide an overview of the principles and practices of artificial intelligence (AI).
- To introduce the main concepts and techniques of AI, such as search, knowledge representation, reasoning, planning, learning, natural language processing, computer vision, and robotics.
- To develop the skills and abilities to apply AI methods and tools to solve real-world problems.
- To foster the critical thinking and ethical awareness of the opportunities and challenges of AI in society.



### 1. To enable the students to understand about the fundamental concepts of analytical instruments

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Analytical instruments can be classified into different categories based on their principles of operation, such as spectroscopy, chromatography, electrochemistry, mass spectrometry, etc.
- Analytical instruments can also be classified based on their applications, such as environmental, clinical, forensic, pharmaceutical, food, etc.
- Analytical instruments can provide qualitative or quantitative information about the samples, such as their identity, composition, structure, concentration, purity, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different components, such as sources, detectors, transducers, signal processors, data analyzers, etc.
- Analytical instruments can have different advantages and limitations, such as sensitivity, selectivity, accuracy, precision, speed, cost, etc.
- Analytical instruments can follow different standards and protocols, such as calibration, validation, quality control, etc.



### 2. To enable the students to understand about the analysis of chloride content, hardness, alkalinity of water.

- Chloride content is the amount of chloride ions (Cl-) present in water. It is an indicator of water quality and salinity. Chloride content can be measured by titrating a water sample with silver nitrate (AgNO3) solution using potassium chromate (K2CrO4) as an indicator. The end point is marked by the formation of a reddish-brown precipitate of silver chromate (Ag2CrO4).
- Hardness is the property of water that causes the formation of scale or scum when water is heated or when soap is added. It is mainly caused by the presence of calcium (Ca2+) and magnesium (Mg2+) ions in water. Hardness can be classified into temporary hardness and permanent hardness. Temporary hardness is due to the presence of bicarbonates of calcium and magnesium, which can be removed by boiling the water. Permanent hardness is due to the presence of chlorides, sulfates, and nitrates of calcium and magnesium, which cannot be removed by boiling. Hardness can be measured by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) using eriochrome black T as an indicator. The end point is marked by a color change from wine red to blue.
- Alkalinity is the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of sodium, potassium, calcium, and magnesium in water. Alkalinity can be measured by titrating a water sample with a standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) using phenolphthalein and methyl orange as indicators. The end point with phenolphthalein is marked by a color change from pink to colorless, indicating the amount of hydroxides and half of the carbonates. The end point with methyl orange is marked by a color change from yellow to orange, indicating the amount of bicarbonates and the remaining carbonates.



### 3. To enable the students to understand about the measure of pH, surface tension and viscosity of a liquid.

- pH is a measure of how acidic or basic a liquid is. It is defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration in moles per liter. The pH scale ranges from 0 to 14, where 7 is neutral, lower than 7 is acidic, and higher than 7 is basic. The pH of a liquid can be measured using indicators, such as litmus paper, or pH meters, which use electrodes to measure the electric potential difference between the liquid and a reference solution.
- Surface tension is a measure of how strongly the molecules of a liquid are attracted to each other at the surface. It is defined as the amount of force per unit length required to break or stretch the surface of a liquid. The surface tension of a liquid depends on the intermolecular forces, the temperature, and the presence of surfactants, which are substances that reduce the surface tension. The surface tension of a liquid can be measured using methods, such as the drop weight method, the ring method, or the pendant drop method, which involve measuring the shape or size of a drop of liquid.
- Viscosity is a measure of how resistant a liquid is to flow. It is defined as the ratio of the shear stress to the shear rate in a liquid. The shear stress is the force per unit area applied tangentially to the liquid, and the shear rate is the rate of change of the velocity of the liquid in the direction perpendicular to the force. The viscosity of a liquid depends on the intermolecular forces, the temperature, and the pressure. The viscosity of a liquid can be measured using methods, such as the capillary tube method, the falling ball method, or the rotational viscometer method, which involve measuring the flow rate or the torque of a liquid.



### 4. To enable the students to understand about the preparation of different resins.

- Resins are natural or synthetic organic substances that are usually solid or semi-solid, and have a high molecular weight and a sticky or hard texture.
- Resins are widely used in various industries, such as paints, adhesives, plastics, rubber, cosmetics, pharmaceuticals, and electronics.
- Resins can be classified into two main types: thermoplastic and thermosetting.
- Thermoplastic resins are resins that can be melted and reshaped by heating, such as polyethylene, polypropylene, polystyrene, and nylon.
- Thermosetting resins are resins that undergo irreversible chemical reactions when heated, and form cross-linked networks that cannot be remelted, such as epoxy, phenolic, polyester, and urea-formaldehyde resins.
- The preparation of different resins involves various steps, such as polymerization, condensation, cross-linking, curing, and modification.
- Polymerization is the process of joining small molecules (monomers) to form large molecules (polymers) by covalent bonds, such as the formation of polyethylene from ethylene monomers.
- Condensation is the process of joining two molecules with the elimination of a small molecule, such as water, alcohol, or ammonia, such as the formation of nylon from hexamethylene diamine and adipic acid.
- Cross-linking is the process of forming covalent bonds between different polymer chains, which increases the rigidity and strength of the resin, such as the formation of vulcanized rubber from natural rubber and sulfur.
- Curing is the process of hardening and stabilizing the resin by applying heat, pressure, catalysts, or radiation, which completes the cross-linking and eliminates any volatile components, such as the formation of epoxy resin from epichlorohydrin and bisphenol A.
- Modification is the process of changing the properties of the resin by adding additives, fillers, or other polymers, which can improve the performance, appearance, or compatibility of the resin, such as the formation of ABS (acrylonitrile butadiene styrene) resin from styrene, acrylonitrile, and polybutadiene.



### 5. To enable the students to understand about the synthesis of organic compounds such as adipic acid and paracetamol by conventional and green route.

- Adipic acid is a dicarboxylic acid that is used as a raw material for the production of nylon-6,6, a synthetic polymer widely used in various applications.
- Paracetamol is an analgesic and antipyretic drug that is widely used to relieve pain and fever.
- Conventional route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexane or a mixture of cyclohexanol and cyclohexanone with nitric acid, which produces large amounts of nitrous oxide, a greenhouse gas and an ozone depleter.
- Conventional route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the acetylation of phenol with acetic anhydride, which produces large amounts of acetic acid, a corrosive and toxic by-product.
- Green route of synthesis of adipic acid involves the oxidation of cyclohexene with hydrogen peroxide in a microemulsion, in the presence of a tungsten-based catalyst and a surfactant, which produces pure adipic acid in high yield and allows the recycling of the catalyst and the surfactant.
- Green route of synthesis of paracetamol involves the reaction of 4-aminophenol with acetic acid in water, in the presence of a microwave irradiation, which produces paracetamol in high yield and avoids the use of harmful organic solvents and acetic anhydride.
- The advantages of the green route of synthesis of adipic acid and paracetamol are:
  - They reduce the environmental impact of the chemical processes by minimizing the use of hazardous reagents, solvents, and catalysts, and by avoiding the generation of harmful by-products and emissions.
  - They improve the economic efficiency of the chemical processes by increasing the yield and selectivity of the desired products, and by reducing the energy consumption and the waste disposal costs.
  - They enhance the social responsibility of the chemical industry by promoting the principles of green chemistry and sustainable development, and by improving the health and safety of the workers and the consumers.



## LIST OF EXPERIMENTS

- An experiment is a scientific procedure that aims to test a hypothesis, answer a question, or discover something new.
- Experiments usually involve manipulating one or more variables, measuring their effects, and comparing them with a control group.
- Experiments can be classified into different types based on their purpose, design, and method of data analysis.
- Some common types of experiments are:

  - **Randomized controlled trial (RCT)**: An experiment that randomly assigns participants to either an experimental group that receives a treatment or intervention, or a control group that does not. The outcome of interest is then measured and compared between the two groups. RCTs are often used to test the effectiveness and safety of medical treatments, drugs, or policies.
  - **Factorial design**: An experiment that involves two or more independent variables, each with two or more levels, and examines their combined effects on a dependent variable. For example, a factorial design can test how different doses of a drug and different types of exercise affect blood pressure.
  - **Quasi-experiment**: An experiment that lacks random assignment of participants to groups, but still compares the effects of a treatment or intervention on a dependent variable. For example, a quasi-experiment can compare the academic performance of students who attend a new curriculum versus those who attend the old one, without randomly assigning them to the groups.
  - **Natural experiment**: An experiment that exploits a naturally occurring event or situation that creates a difference in the exposure or treatment of participants, and compares the outcomes between the groups. For example, a natural experiment can compare the health effects of living near a nuclear plant versus living far away, using the plant's location as the source of variation.
  - **Observational study**: A study that does not involve any manipulation of variables, but only observes and measures the characteristics and outcomes of participants. For example, an observational study can examine the relationship between smoking and lung cancer, without intervening or controlling any factors.
  - **Survey**: A study that collects data from a sample of individuals using questionnaires, interviews, or other methods of measurement. Surveys can be used to describe the characteristics, opinions, behaviors, or attitudes of a population, or to test hypotheses about them.



### 1. Calibration of Analytical Equipment and apparatus

- Calibration is the process of evaluating and adjusting the precision and accuracy of measurement equipment.
- Calibration ensures that the equipment and apparatus produce valid and reliable results that are consistent with the standards and specifications.
- Calibration is required for all analytical equipment and apparatus that are used for quantitative measurements, such as balances, pipettes, thermometers, spectrophotometers, pH meters, etc.
- Calibration should be performed at regular intervals, depending on the frequency of use, the stability of the equipment, the manufacturer's recommendations, and the in-house standard operating procedures.
- Calibration should be done by qualified and trained personnel, using appropriate reference standards and methods.
- Calibration should be documented and recorded, with the date, time, results, adjustments, and signatures of the personnel involved.
- Calibration should be verified by using check standards or quality control samples before and after the calibration.
- Calibration should be repeated if the equipment or apparatus is repaired, modified, relocated, or suspected of malfunctioning.



### 2. Determination of Hardness of water sample by EDTA method

- Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water.
- EDTA (Ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid) is a chelating agent that can form stable complexes with metal ions, such as Ca2+ and Mg2+.
- EDTA titration is a method of determining the hardness of water by titrating the water sample with a standard solution of EDTA in the presence of a suitable indicator.
- The indicator used is Eriochrome Black T (EBT), which is a dye that changes color from wine red to blue when it binds to metal ions.
- The procedure for EDTA titration is as follows:

  - Take a sample volume of 20 ml (V ml) of water in an Erlenmeyer flask.
  - Dilute the sample to 40 ml by adding 20 ml of distilled water.
  - Add 1 ml of ammonia buffer to adjust the pH to 10 ± 0.1.
  - Add 1 or 2 drops of EBT indicator solution. The solution will turn wine red due to the formation of EBT-metal complexes.
  - Titrate the sample with a standard solution of EDTA (M molarity) from a burette with vigorous shaking until the color changes from wine red to blue. This indicates the end point of the titration.
  - Record the volume of EDTA used (V1 ml).

- The calculation of hardness of water by EDTA titration is based on the following equation:

  - M * V1 = V * (C1 + C2)

  - Where M is the molarity of EDTA, V1 is the volume of EDTA used, V is the volume of water sample, C1 is the concentration of Ca2+ in water, and C2 is the concentration of Mg2+ in water.

  - The total hardness of water (TH) is the sum of C1 and C2, expressed in mg/L as CaCO3.

  - The total hardness of water (TH) can be calculated as:

    - TH = M * V1 * 1000 / V

  - The calcium hardness of water (CH) is the concentration of Ca2+ in water, expressed in mg/L as CaCO3.

  - The calcium hardness of water (CH) can be calculated as:

    - CH = M * V1 * 1000 / V * 50 / 100

  - The magnesium hardness of water (MH) is the concentration of Mg2+ in water, expressed in mg/L as CaCO3.

  - The magnesium hardness of water (MH) can be calculated as:

    - MH = TH - CH

- The results of EDTA titration can be reported as:

  - Total Hardness (EDTA) = TH mg/L as CaCO3
  - Calcium Hardness (EDTA) = CH mg/L as CaCO3
  - Magnesium Hardness (EDTA) = MH mg/L as CaCO3



### 3. Determination of Alkalinity of water sample.

- Alkalinity is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly caused by the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates, and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium, and other metals in water.
- Alkalinity is important for water quality because it affects the pH, corrosion, and disinfection processes of water. High alkalinity can cause scaling and reduce the effectiveness of chlorine. Low alkalinity can cause corrosion and pH fluctuations.
- Alkalinity can be determined by titrating a water sample with a standard acid solution, such as sulfuric acid or hydrochloric acid, using a suitable indicator, such as phenolphthalein or methyl orange, to detect the endpoint of the titration.
- The procedure for determining alkalinity of water sample is as follows:

  - Take a 100 mL aliquot of the water sample in a conical flask and add 2-3 drops of phenolphthalein indicator. If the sample turns pink, it indicates the presence of phenolphthalein alkalinity (P-alkalinity), which is due to hydroxides and half of the carbonates. Record the initial burette reading of the standard acid solution.
  - Titrate the sample with the standard acid solution until the pink color disappears. Record the final burette reading and calculate the volume of acid used. This volume corresponds to the P-alkalinity of the sample.
  - Add 2-3 drops of methyl orange indicator to the same sample and continue the titration with the standard acid solution until the color changes from yellow to orange. Record the final burette reading and calculate the volume of acid used. This volume corresponds to the total alkalinity (T-alkalinity) of the sample, which is due to hydroxides, carbonates, and bicarbonates.
  - The P-alkalinity and T-alkalinity can be expressed in terms of milligrams per liter (mg/L) of calcium carbonate (CaCO3) equivalent by multiplying the volume of acid used by the normality of the acid and by a factor of 50.
  - The types and amounts of alkalinity present in the sample can be determined by using the following relationships:

    - If P-alkalinity = 0, then T-alkalinity = bicarbonate alkalinity (B-alkalinity).
    - If P-alkalinity = T-alkalinity, then P-alkalinity = hydroxide alkalinity (OH-alkalinity) and B-alkalinity = 0.
    - If P-alkalinity < T-alkalinity, then P-alkalinity = 2 x carbonate alkalinity (C-alkalinity) and B-alkalinity = T-alkalinity - P-alkalinity.
    - If P-alkalinity > T-alkalinity, then the sample contains interfering substances and the results are invalid.



### 4. Determination of pH by titrimetric method

- Titrimetric method is a technique of quantitative analysis that involves measuring the volume of a solution of known concentration (titrant) that is required to react completely with a solution of unknown concentration (analyte)  .
- pH is a measure of the acidity or basicity of a solution, defined as the negative logarithm of the hydrogen ion concentration: pH = -log[H3O+]  .
- Determination of pH by titrimetric method involves using a pH meter to monitor the change in pH of the analyte solution as the titrant is added   .
- A pH meter consists of a glass electrode that responds to the hydrogen ion activity in the solution, and a reference electrode that provides a constant potential. The difference in potential between the two electrodes is proportional to the pH of the solution  .
- A plot of the pH of the analyte solution versus the volume of the titrant added is called a titration curve. The titration curve can be used to determine the endpoint or equivalence point of the titration, where the analyte and the titrant are stoichiometrically equal  .
- The shape and features of the titration curve depend on the type and strength of the acid and base involved in the titration, as well as the buffer capacity of the analyte solution .
- The titration curve can also be used to calculate the pKa or pKb of a weak acid or base, by finding the pH at the half-equivalence point, where the concentration of the weak acid or base is equal to the concentration of its conjugate base or acid .



### 5. Determination of surface tension of given liquid.

- Surface tension is a property of liquids that causes them to behave as if they have a thin elastic skin on their surface.
- Surface tension is caused by the cohesive forces between the molecules of the liquid, which tend to minimize the surface area of the liquid.
- Surface tension can be measured by various methods, such as the capillary rise method, the drop weight method, the ring method, and the stalagmometer method.
- In this experiment, we will use the stalagmometer method to determine the surface tension of a given liquid.
- A stalagmometer is a device that consists of a glass tube with a bulb at one end and a fine nozzle at the other end.
- The tube is filled with the liquid to be tested and then inverted over a graduated cylinder.
- The liquid drips out of the nozzle and the number of drops that fall in a certain volume of the cylinder is counted.
- The surface tension of the liquid is proportional to the weight of each drop, which can be calculated from the density of the liquid and the volume of the cylinder.
- The formula for the surface tension of the liquid is:

$$\gamma = \frac{4}{3} \pi r^3 \rho g \frac{N_1}{N_2}$$

where $\gamma$ is the surface tension, $r$ is the radius of the nozzle, $\rho$ is the density of the liquid, $g$ is the acceleration due to gravity, $N_1$ is the number of drops of the liquid, and $N_2$ is the number of drops of a standard liquid (such as water or alcohol) with known surface tension.



### 6. Determination of Viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer.

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow or deform under an applied force.
- A viscometer is a device that measures the viscosity of a fluid by observing its flow through a narrow tube or a capillary.
- The flow rate of a fluid through a capillary depends on the pressure difference across the capillary, the length and radius of the capillary, and the viscosity of the fluid.
- The Hagen-Poiseuille equation relates these parameters as follows:

$$Q = \frac{\pi r^4 \Delta P}{8 \eta L}$$

where Q is the volumetric flow rate, r is the radius of the capillary, $\Delta P$ is the pressure difference, $\eta$ is the viscosity, and L is the length of the capillary.

- To determine the viscosity of a given liquid by viscometer, the following steps are followed:

  - Fill the viscometer with the liquid to be tested and immerse it in a water bath at a constant temperature.
  - Allow the liquid to flow through the capillary under the influence of gravity and measure the time taken for a fixed volume of liquid to flow between two marked points on the viscometer.
  - Repeat the experiment for different volumes of liquid and calculate the average time for each volume.
  - Plot a graph of volume versus time and find the slope of the best-fit line.
  - Use the Hagen-Poiseuille equation to calculate the viscosity of the liquid, knowing the dimensions of the capillary and the pressure difference.
  - Compare the experimental value of viscosity with the theoretical value and calculate the percentage error.



### 7. Determination of the strength of Ferrous ammonium sulfate using external indicator.

- Ferrous ammonium sulfate, also known as Mohr's salt, is a double salt of iron(II) sulfate and ammonium sulfate, with the formula (NH4)2Fe(SO4)2·6H2O.
- It is often used as a standard solution for titrating oxidizing agents, such as potassium permanganate or potassium dichromate, in redox reactions.
- The strength of a ferrous ammonium sulfate solution can be determined by titrating it with a standard solution of potassium permanganate, using an external indicator such as starch or potassium ferricyanide.
- The reaction between ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium permanganate is:

(NH4)2Fe(SO4)2 + KMnO4 + H2SO4 -> Fe2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + (NH4)2SO4 + MnSO4 + H2O

- The reaction between ferrous ammonium sulfate and potassium dichromate is:

(NH4)2Fe(SO4)2 + K2Cr2O7 + H2SO4 -> Fe2(SO4)3 + K2SO4 + (NH4)2SO4 + Cr2(SO4)3 + H2O

- The end point of the titration is detected by the change of color of the external indicator. Starch turns blue in the presence of iodine, which is formed by the reaction of excess potassium permanganate and potassium iodide. Potassium ferricyanide turns brown in the presence of iron(III) ions, which are formed by the oxidation of ferrous ammonium sulfate.
- The procedure for the titration is as follows:

  - Prepare a standard solution of potassium permanganate or potassium dichromate of known concentration and a solution of potassium iodide or potassium ferricyanide as the external indicator.
  - Pipette a known volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution into a conical flask and add some dilute sulfuric acid.
  - Fill a burette with the standard solution of potassium permanganate or potassium dichromate and note the initial reading.
  - Add the external indicator solution to the conical flask and swirl the mixture.
  - Titrate the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution with the standard solution until the color of the external indicator changes.
  - Note the final reading of the burette and calculate the volume of the standard solution used.
  - Repeat the titration until concordant results are obtained.
  - Calculate the strength of the ferrous ammonium sulfate solution using the following formula:

Strength of ferrous ammonium sulfate = (Volume of standard solution x Normality of standard solution x Equivalent weight of ferrous ammonium sulfate) / (Volume of ferrous ammonium sulfate solution x 1000)



### 8. Determination of the strength of Potassium dichromate using internal indicator.

- Potassium dichromate is a primary standard substance that can be used to determine the strength of a solution of an oxidizing or reducing agent.
- The internal indicator is a substance that changes color when the equivalence point is reached in a titration. It is added to the solution being titrated, not to the titrant.
- An example of an internal indicator is diphenylamine, which is used to titrate potassium dichromate against ferrous ammonium sulphate (FAS) solution.
- The procedure for the determination of the strength of potassium dichromate using internal indicator is as follows:

  - Weigh accurately about 0.2 g of potassium dichromate and dissolve it in 100 mL of distilled water in a volumetric flask. This is the standard solution of potassium dichromate.
  - Pipette out 10 mL of FAS solution into a conical flask and add 10 mL of dilute sulphuric acid and a few drops of diphenylamine indicator.
  - Fill a burette with the standard solution of potassium dichromate and note the initial reading.
  - Titrate the FAS solution with the potassium dichromate solution until a faint blue color appears in the solution. This indicates the end point of the titration. Note the final reading of the burette.
  - Repeat the titration for two more times and take the average of the readings.
  - Calculate the strength of potassium dichromate using the formula:

    - Strength of potassium dichromate = (Volume of potassium dichromate x Normality of potassium dichromate x Equivalent weight of potassium dichromate) / (Volume of FAS x Equivalent weight of FAS)
    - Normality of potassium dichromate = (Weight of potassium dichromate x 1000) / (Molecular weight of potassium dichromate x Volume of potassium dichromate)
    - Equivalent weight of potassium dichromate = Molecular weight of potassium dichromate / 6
    - Equivalent weight of FAS = Molecular weight of FAS / 1
    - Molecular weight of potassium dichromate = 294.2 g/mol
    - Molecular weight of FAS = 392.2 g/mol



### 9. Determination of available chlorine in bleaching powder.

- Bleaching powder is a compound of calcium hypochlorite (Ca(OCl)2), calcium chloride (CaCl2), and calcium hydroxide (Ca(OH)2).
- Available chlorine is the amount of chlorine that can be liberated from bleaching powder by the action of acids.
- The available chlorine in bleaching powder can be determined by iodometric titration, which involves the following steps:

  - A known mass of bleaching powder is dissolved in water and filtered to remove any insoluble impurities.
  - A measured volume of the filtrate is acidified with dilute hydrochloric acid, which causes the release of chlorine gas from the hypochlorite ions.
  - The chlorine gas reacts with potassium iodide (KI) solution, which is present in excess, to form iodine (I2) and potassium chloride (KCl).
  - The liberated iodine is titrated with standard sodium thiosulfate (Na2S2O3) solution, using starch as an indicator, until the blue color disappears.
  - The amount of sodium thiosulfate used is proportional to the amount of iodine, which is in turn proportional to the amount of chlorine, which is in turn proportional to the amount of available chlorine in the bleaching powder.
  - The percentage of available chlorine in the bleaching powder can be calculated using the following formula:

    % available chlorine = (V x N x 35.5 x 100) / (W x 1000)

    where V is the volume of sodium thiosulfate solution used in mL, N is the normality of sodium thiosulfate solution, W is the mass of bleaching powder taken in g, and 35.5 is the equivalent mass of chlorine.



### 10. Determination of chloride content in water sample.

- Chloride is one of the major anions found in natural waters. It originates from various sources, such as seawater intrusion, industrial effluents, agricultural runoff, and domestic sewage.
- Chloride can affect the taste, corrosion, and biological activity of water. Therefore, it is important to monitor its concentration in drinking water, wastewater, and surface water.
- The most common method for determining chloride content in water sample is the **argentometric method**. It is based on the precipitation of chloride ions with silver ions in the presence of a suitable indicator.
- The procedure for the argentometric method is as follows:

  - Take a known volume of water sample in a conical flask and add a few drops of potassium chromate indicator. The indicator will give a yellow color to the solution.
  - Titrate the solution with standard silver nitrate solution from a burette until a permanent brick-red precipitate of silver chromate appears. This indicates the end point of the titration.
  - Record the volume of silver nitrate solution used and calculate the chloride content in the water sample using the following formula:

    - Chloride content (mg/L) = (Volume of AgNO3 x Normality of AgNO3 x 35.45) / Volume of water sample

- The advantages of the argentometric method are:

  - It is simple, rapid, and accurate.
  - It does not require sophisticated instruments or reagents.
  - It can be applied to a wide range of chloride concentrations.

- The limitations of the argentometric method are:

  - It is not suitable for water samples that contain interfering substances, such as bromide, iodide, cyanide, sulfide, or thiocyanate. These substances can also precipitate with silver ions and cause errors in the results.
  - It is affected by the presence of carbonate and bicarbonate in the water sample. These substances can react with silver ions and form insoluble silver carbonate, which can mask the end point of the titration. To avoid this, the water sample should be acidified with nitric acid before the titration.
  - It requires a careful selection of the indicator. The indicator should have a sharp color change at the end point and should not react with silver ions or chloride ions. Potassium chromate is the most commonly used indicator, but it can also be replaced by other indicators, such as fluorescein, dichlorofluorescein, or ferricyanide.



### 11. Preparation of Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin

- Phenol formaldehyde (PF) resin is a type of thermosetting resin that is obtained by the polymerization of phenol and formaldehyde.
- Phenol and formaldehyde react in a step-growth polymerization reaction that can be either acid- or base-catalyzed .
- The ratio of phenol to formaldehyde affects the structure and properties of the resin. A 1:1 ratio produces linear chains, while a higher ratio of formaldehyde produces cross-linked networks.
- The reaction can be carried out in one or two stages. In the one-stage process, phenol and formaldehyde are mixed with a catalyst and heated under reflux until the desired degree of polymerization is reached. In the two-stage process, a low-molecular-weight resin (called a resol) is first prepared by reacting phenol and formaldehyde under alkaline conditions, and then cured by adding an acid catalyst and heating .
- The properties of PF resin depend on the type and amount of catalyst, the reaction temperature and time, the phenol to formaldehyde ratio, and the degree of cross-linking. PF resin is hard, brittle, heat-resistant, and has good electrical insulation.
- PF resin is widely used in the manufacture of plywood, particleboard, laminates, molding compounds, coatings, adhesives, and electrical components.



### 12. Preparation of Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin

- Urea formaldehyde (UF) resin is a thermosetting polymer that is widely used in adhesives, plywood, particle board, medium-density fibreboard (MDF), and molded objects.
- UF resin is synthesized by the condensation reaction of urea and formaldehyde in the presence of a base or an acid catalyst .
- The reaction can be divided into three steps: methylolation, condensation, and curing .
- Methylolation: In this step, formaldehyde reacts with urea to form monomethylolurea and dimethylolurea, which are the monomers of UF resin . The reaction is carried out in an aqueous solution containing more than 50% formaldehyde and urea in a molar ratio of 2-3:1 at pH 6-11 . The reaction is exothermic and requires heating to at least 80°C .
- Condensation: In this step, the monomers undergo further polymerization to form low molecular weight prepolymers with a high degree of branching . The reaction is catalyzed by an acid, such as a mineral or organic acid, which is added to bring the pH to 0.5-3.5 . The reaction is also exothermic and requires heating to 90-100°C .
- Curing: In this step, the prepolymers crosslink to form a three-dimensional network of UF resin with high strength and rigidity . The reaction is catalyzed by heat, pressure, or a hardener, such as ammonia, which is added to the resin . The reaction is endothermic and requires heating to 120-150°C .
- The properties and performance of UF resin depend on the reaction conditions, such as the molar ratio of formaldehyde to urea, the pH, the temperature, the catalyst, and the hardener .
- UF resin has some advantages, such as low cost, fast curing, high water resistance, and good adhesion to wood .
- UF resin also has some disadvantages, such as low thermal stability, poor weather resistance, and high formaldehyde emission, which can cause health and environmental problems .

### 14. Determination of Cell Conductance of a solution.

- Cell conductance is the measure of the ability of a solution to conduct electric current through it.
- Cell conductance depends on the concentration, type and mobility of the ions present in the solution, as well as the temperature and geometry of the cell.
- Cell conductance is measured by applying an alternating current (AC) of known frequency and amplitude to a cell containing the solution, and measuring the potential difference across the cell terminals.
- Cell conductance is calculated by using Ohm's law: `G = I / V`, where `G` is the cell conductance, `I` is the current, and `V` is the potential difference.
- Cell conductance is expressed in units of siemens (S) or mho (℧), which are equivalent to the reciprocal of ohm (Ω).
- Cell conductance is related to the cell resistance by the formula: `G = 1 / R`, where `R` is the cell resistance.
- Cell conductance is also related to the specific conductance or conductivity of the solution by the formula: `G = k * A / l`, where `k` is the specific conductance, `A` is the cross-sectional area of the cell, and `l` is the length of the cell.
- Specific conductance is a characteristic property of the solution, independent of the cell dimensions, and is expressed in units of siemens per meter (S/m) or mho per centimeter (℧/cm).
- Specific conductance can be used to determine the concentration of an electrolyte solution, by using a calibration curve or a conductometric titration.

### 15. Determination of Rate constant of hydrolysis of esters

- Esters are organic compounds that have the general formula RCOOR', where R and R' are alkyl or aryl groups.
- Esters can undergo hydrolysis, which is a chemical reaction with water, to produce carboxylic acids and alcohols.
- The hydrolysis of esters can be catalyzed by acids or bases, or can occur spontaneously under certain conditions.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters depends on several factors, such as the nature of the ester, the concentration of the reactants, the temperature, and the presence of catalysts.
- The rate of hydrolysis of esters can be expressed by the following rate law:

  - Rate = k [Ester] [Water]

  - Where k is the rate constant, [Ester] and [Water] are the molar concentrations of the ester and water, respectively.
- The rate constant k can be determined experimentally by measuring the rate of hydrolysis of a known concentration of ester at a constant temperature and pressure.
- One method to measure the rate of hydrolysis of esters is to use a conductometric titration, which is a technique that measures the change in electrical conductivity of a solution as a function of the amount of titrant added.
- In a conductometric titration, the ester is hydrolyzed by a known concentration of a strong base, such as sodium hydroxide, and the resulting carboxylic acid is titrated by a known concentration of a strong acid, such as hydrochloric acid.
- The conductivity of the solution changes as the hydrolysis and titration reactions proceed, and the change in conductivity can be plotted against the volume of acid added.
- The plot of conductivity versus volume of acid added is called a conductometric titration curve, and it has the following features:

  - The initial conductivity is high, because the solution contains a high concentration of ester and base ions.
  - The conductivity decreases as the ester is hydrolyzed and the base is consumed, forming a carboxylic acid and an alcohol.
  - The conductivity reaches a minimum at the equivalence point, where the amount of acid added is equal to the amount of base consumed.
  - The conductivity increases again as the excess acid is added, forming more hydronium ions in the solution.
- The rate constant k can be calculated from the conductometric titration curve by using the following steps:

  - Determine the initial concentration of the ester and the base from the initial conductivity and the calibration curve of the conductivity meter.
  - Determine the concentration of the ester and the base at any point on the titration curve by using the stoichiometry of the hydrolysis and titration reactions.
  - Determine the rate of hydrolysis of the ester at any point on the titration curve by using the rate law and the concentrations of the ester and water.
  - Plot the natural logarithm of the rate of hydrolysis versus the time elapsed from the start of the titration.
  - The slope of the plot is equal to -k, and the intercept is equal to ln k.
  - The value of k can be obtained by taking the antilogarithm of the intercept.



### 16. Element detection and identification of functional groups in organic compounds

- Element detection is the process of determining the presence and quantity of elements in an organic compound, such as carbon, hydrogen, nitrogen, oxygen, halogens, sulfur, and phosphorus.
- Identification of functional groups is the process of determining the type and location of the specific groups of atoms that are responsible for the characteristic chemical reactions of an organic compound, such as alcohols, aldehydes, ketones, carboxylic acids, amines, etc.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups are important for the analysis and classification of organic compounds, as they provide information about their structure, properties, and reactivity.
- Element detection and identification of functional groups can be done by various methods, such as:
  - Physical methods, such as solubility, melting point, boiling point, refractive index, etc.
  - Chemical methods, such as combustion analysis, Lassaigne's test, sodium fusion test, etc.
  - Spectroscopic methods, such as mass spectrometry, infrared spectroscopy, nuclear magnetic resonance spectroscopy, etc.
- Some examples of chemical tests for the detection of elements and functional groups in organic compounds are:
  - Carbon and hydrogen: These elements can be detected by heating the organic compound with copper oxide in a test tube. The carbon is oxidized to carbon dioxide, which turns lime water milky, and the hydrogen is oxidized to water, which condenses on the cooler part of the test tube.
  - Nitrogen: This element can be detected by Lassaigne's test, which involves heating the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The nitrogen is converted to sodium cyanide, which can be extracted with water and tested with ferrous sulfate and ferric chloride solutions. The formation of a Prussian blue precipitate indicates the presence of nitrogen.
  - Oxygen: This element can be detected by heating the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The oxygen is converted to sodium oxide, which can be extracted with water and tested with phenolphthalein indicator. The formation of a pink color indicates the presence of oxygen.
  - Halogens: These elements can be detected by sodium fusion test, which involves heating the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The halogens are converted to sodium halides, which can be extracted with water and tested with silver nitrate solution. The formation of a white, cream, or yellow precipitate indicates the presence of chlorine, bromine, or iodine, respectively.
  - Sulfur: This element can be detected by sodium fusion test, which involves heating the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The sulfur is converted to sodium sulfide, which can be extracted with water and tested with lead acetate solution. The formation of a black precipitate indicates the presence of sulfur.
  - Phosphorus: This element can be detected by sodium fusion test, which involves heating the organic compound with sodium metal in a fusion tube. The phosphorus is converted to sodium phosphate, which can be extracted with water and tested with ammonium molybdate solution. The formation of a yellow precipitate indicates the presence of phosphorus.
  - Alcoholic –OH group: This functional group can be detected by the following tests:
    - Lucas test: This test involves adding a few drops of the organic compound to a mixture of zinc chloride and concentrated hydrochloric acid in a test tube. The formation of a turbid solution or a separate layer indicates the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. The rate of turbidity depends on the type of alcohol: primary alcohols give turbidity after several minutes, secondary alcohols give turbidity within a few minutes, and tertiary alcohols give turbidity immediately.
    - Esterification test: This test involves adding a few drops of the organic compound to a mixture of acetic acid and concentrated sulfuric acid in a test tube. The formation of a fruity smell indicates the presence of an alcoholic –OH group. The smell is due to the formation of an ester by the reaction of the alcohol and the acetic acid.
  - Carbonyls (Aldehydes and Ketones): These functional groups can be detected by the following tests:
    - 2,4-dinitrophenyl hydrazine test: This test involves adding a small amount (2 drops or 0.05 – 0.1g) of the organic compound to 3 ml of 2,4-dinitrophenyl hydrazine reagent and shaking well. The formation

### Course Outcomes Bloom's Taxonomy

- Course outcomes are brief statements that describe what students will be expected to learn by the end of the course.
- Bloom's taxonomy is a model of cognitive skills used to classify educational learning objectives and is a helpful tool for the development of learning outcomes.
- Bloom's taxonomy consists of six levels of thinking, from lower to higher order: remember, understand, apply, analyze, evaluate, and create.
- Course outcomes should be aligned with the level of thinking that the course intends to foster in students.
- Course outcomes should use verbs that reflect the level of thinking, such as describe, explain, compare, contrast, solve, design, etc.
- Course outcomes should be specific, measurable, achievable, relevant, and time-bound.
- Course outcomes should be communicated to students at the beginning of the course and assessed throughout the course.

Below are some examples of course outcomes that utilize Bloom's taxonomy:

- At the end of the course, students will be able to **describe** the colonization of the Americas by the British, French and Spanish (remember level).
- At the end of the course, students will be able to **explain** the main concepts and theories of social psychology (understand level).
- At the end of the course, students will be able to **apply** the principles of accounting to prepare financial statements (apply level).
- At the end of the course, students will be able to **analyze** the outcomes of the Civil War using primary and secondary sources (analyze level).
- At the end of the course, students will be able to **evaluate** the ethical implications of genetic engineering (evaluate level).
- At the end of the course, students will be able to **create** a portfolio of original poems that demonstrate their mastery of poetic techniques (create level).



### Level

- A level is a measure of the amount or degree of something, such as height, quantity, quality, or intensity.
- Levels can be expressed in various units, such as meters, liters, decibels, or degrees Celsius.
- Levels can be compared using words like higher, lower, equal, or different.
- Levels can be used to classify or rank things into categories, such as beginner, intermediate, or advanced.
- Levels can be used to describe the progress or achievement of a goal, such as passing a test, completing a course, or reaching a milestone.
- Levels can be used to indicate the difficulty or complexity of a task, such as easy, medium, or hard.
- Levels can be used to show the structure or hierarchy of a system, such as top, middle, or bottom.



### CO-1 Get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments. K3

- Analytical instruments are devices that are used to measure, analyze, or monitor physical, chemical, or biological properties of samples.
- Different analytical instruments have different applications, principles, and techniques, depending on the type of sample and the property of interest.
- Some examples of analytical instruments are:

  - Spectrophotometers: measure the amount of light absorbed or emitted by a sample at different wavelengths.
  - Chromatographs: separate the components of a mixture based on their affinity to a stationary phase or a mobile phase.
  - Mass spectrometers: identify the molecular mass and structure of a sample by ionizing it and detecting the resulting fragments.
  - Electrochemical analyzers: measure the electrical potential or current of a sample in relation to a reference electrode or a redox reaction.
  - Microscopes: magnify the image of a sample using light, electrons, or other forms of radiation.
  - Biosensors: detect the presence or concentration of a specific biomolecule or analyte in a sample using a biological recognition element and a transducer.

- To get an understanding of the use of different analytical instruments, one should:

  - Know the basic principles and techniques of each instrument and how they relate to the sample and the property of interest.
  - Know the advantages and limitations of each instrument and how to select the most appropriate one for a given analytical problem.
  - Know how to operate, calibrate, and maintain each instrument and how to interpret and report the results obtained.
  - Know how to apply quality control and assurance methods to ensure the reliability and accuracy of the measurements.
  - Know how to troubleshoot and solve common problems or errors that may arise during the use of each instrument.



### CO2 Molecular Properties and Surface Tension

- Carbon dioxide (CO2) is a linear molecule with a bond length of 116.3 pm and a bond angle of 180 degrees.
- CO2 is a non-polar molecule with a dipole moment of zero and a symmetrical distribution of electrons.
- CO2 has a molar mass of 44.0095 g/mol and a critical point at 304.13 K and 7.38 MPa.
- CO2 is a gas at standard temperature and pressure, but it can be liquefied under high pressure or low temperature.
- The density and specific weight of CO2 depend on the temperature and pressure, and can be calculated using online tools or tables.
- The surface tension of CO2 is the energy required to increase the surface area of the liquid phase due to intermolecular forces.
- The surface tension of CO2 decreases with increasing temperature and pressure, as the intermolecular forces become weaker .
- The surface tension of CO2 can be measured using various methods, such as the pendant drop method, the Wilhelmy plate method, or the capillary rise method .
- The surface tension of CO2 is influenced by the presence of solutes, such as monoethanolamine (MEA), which can reduce the surface tension by disrupting the intermolecular forces .



### Viscosity, Conductance of Solution, Chloride and Iron Content in the Water

- Viscosity is a measure of the resistance of a fluid to flow. It depends on the intermolecular forces, temperature, and composition of the fluid. Viscosity decreases with increasing temperature and decreases with increasing solute concentration. Viscosity affects the transport of mass, heat, and momentum in fluids.
- Conductance of solution is a measure of the ability of a solution to conduct electric current. It depends on the concentration, type, and mobility of the ions in the solution, as well as the temperature and the geometry of the electrodes. Conductance increases with increasing ion concentration and mobility, and decreases with increasing viscosity.
- Chloride and iron content in the water are indicators of the quality and purity of the water. Chloride ions are present in seawater, brackish water, and some groundwater sources. They can affect the taste, corrosion, and conductivity of the water. Iron ions are present in some groundwater sources, especially in areas with iron-rich rocks. They can cause staining, discoloration, and taste problems in the water. Chloride and iron content can be measured by various methods, such as titration, colorimetry, spectrophotometry, or ion chromatography .



### K3

- K3 is a type of visa that allows a foreign spouse of a U.S. citizen to enter and reside in the United States while waiting for the approval of their immigrant visa petition.
- K3 visa holders are eligible to apply for a work permit and travel authorization while in the United States.
- K3 visa is valid for two years and can be extended if the immigrant visa process is not completed within that time.
- K3 visa is not a direct path to permanent residency or citizenship. K3 visa holders must still go through the adjustment of status process or consular processing to obtain a green card.
- K3 visa is intended to reduce the separation time between the U.S. citizen and their foreign spouse, but it is not always faster or easier than the immigrant visa process.
- K3 visa requires the U.S. citizen to file two petitions: Form I-130 for the immigrant visa and Form I-129F for the K3 visa. The foreign spouse must also apply for the K3 visa at a U.S. consulate abroad and undergo a medical exam and an interview.
- K3 visa may be terminated if the U.S. citizen withdraws the petitions, the marriage ends, the foreign spouse adjusts status or obtains an immigrant visa, or the foreign spouse violates the terms of the visa.



### CO-3 Measure the hardness and alkalinity of the water. K3

- Hardness of water is the measure of the concentration of calcium and magnesium ions in water. It is expressed in terms of equivalent calcium carbonate (CaCO3).
- Alkalinity of water is the measure of the capacity of water to neutralize acids. It is mainly due to the presence of bicarbonates, carbonates and hydroxides of calcium, magnesium and other metals. It is expressed in terms of equivalent calcium carbonate (CaCO3) as well.
- To measure the hardness of water, the following steps are followed:
  - A standard solution of ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid (EDTA) is prepared and its concentration is determined by titrating it against a standard solution of calcium carbonate.
  - A known volume of water sample is taken in a conical flask and a few drops of eriochrome black T indicator are added. The indicator turns the solution wine red.
  - The EDTA solution is added from a burette until the color changes from wine red to blue, indicating the end point of the titration.
  - The volume of EDTA used is noted and the hardness of water is calculated using the formula:

    Hardness (mg/L as CaCO3) = (Volume of EDTA x Normality of EDTA x 1000) / Volume of water sample

- To measure the alkalinity of water, the following steps are followed:
  - A standard solution of sulfuric acid (H2SO4) is prepared and its concentration is determined by titrating it against a standard solution of sodium carbonate (Na2CO3).
  - A known volume of water sample is taken in a conical flask and a few drops of phenolphthalein indicator are added. The indicator turns the solution pink if the pH is above 8.3.
  - The H2SO4 solution is added from a burette until the color changes from pink to colorless, indicating the end point of the first titration. The volume of H2SO4 used is noted and the phenolphthalein alkalinity (P-alkalinity) is calculated using the formula:

    P-alkalinity (mg/L as CaCO3) = (Volume of H2SO4 x Normality of H2SO4 x 1000 x 50) / Volume of water sample

  - A few drops of methyl orange indicator are added to the same flask. The indicator turns the solution orange if the pH is between 3.1 and 4.4.
  - The H2SO4 solution is added from the same burette until the color changes from orange to red, indicating the end point of the second titration. The total volume of H2SO4 used is noted and the methyl orange alkalinity (M-alkalinity) is calculated using the formula:

    M-alkalinity (mg/L as CaCO3) = (Total volume of H2SO4 x Normality of H2SO4 x 1000 x 50) / Volume of water sample

- The total alkalinity of water is the sum of P-alkalinity and M-alkalinity. It can also be calculated using the formula:

    Total alkalinity (mg/L as CaCO3) = (Volume of H2SO4 x Normality of H2SO4 x 1000 x 100) / Volume of water sample

- The types of alkalinity of water are:
  - Phenolphthalein alkalinity: It is due to the presence of hydroxides and half of the carbonates in water. It is also called hydroxide-carbonate alkalinity.
  - Methyl orange alkalinity: It is due to the presence of carbonates and bicarbonates in water. It is also called carbonate-bicarbonate alkalinity.
  - Total alkalinity: It is due to the presence of hydroxides, carbonates and bicarbonates in water. It is also called hydroxide-carbonate-bicarbonate alkalinity.



### CO-4 Know the fundamental concepts of the preparation of phenol

Phenol is an aromatic compound with a hydroxyl group (-OH) attached to a benzene ring. It is also known as carbolic acid or phenic acid. Phenol has antiseptic and disinfectant properties and is used in the manufacture of plastics, dyes, explosives, and drugs.

There are several methods to prepare phenol from different sources, such as haloarenes, benzene, cumene, diazonium salts, and coal tar. Some of the common methods are:

- **From haloarenes:** Haloarenes are aromatic compounds with a halogen atom (-X) attached to a benzene ring. Examples of haloarenes are chlorobenzene, bromobenzene, and iodobenzene. When haloarenes are fused with sodium hydroxide at high temperature and pressure, sodium phenoxide is produced. Sodium phenoxide is then acidified with dilute acid or carbon dioxide to give phenol. This reaction is known as the Dow process or the Fries process.

  - Example: Chlorobenzene + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + NaCl
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From benzene:** Benzene is a simple aromatic compound with six carbon atoms and six hydrogen atoms. When benzene is treated with concentrated sulfuric acid, benzene sulfonic acid is formed. Benzene sulfonic acid is then fused with sodium hydroxide to give sodium phenoxide, which is acidified to give phenol. This reaction is known as the Raschig process.

  - Example: Benzene + H2SO4 → Benzene sulfonic acid + H2O
  - Benzene sulfonic acid + NaOH → Sodium phenoxide + Na2SO4
  - Sodium phenoxide + HCl → Phenol + NaCl

- **From cumene:** Cumene is an organic compound with the formula C6H5CH(CH3)2. It is also known as isopropylbenzene. When cumene is oxidized with air, cumene hydroperoxide is formed. Cumene hydroperoxide is then cleaved with dilute acid to give phenol and acetone. This reaction is known as the cumene process or the Hock process.

  - Example: Cumene + O2 → Cumene hydroperoxide
  - Cumene hydroperoxide + H2SO4 → Phenol + Acetone + H2O

- **From diazonium salts:** Diazonium salts are organic compounds with the general formula R-N2+X-, where R is an aryl group and X is a halide ion. They are formed by the reaction of aromatic amines with nitrous acid. When diazonium salts are treated with water, they undergo hydrolysis to give phenol and nitrogen gas. This reaction is known as the Sandmeyer reaction.

  - Example: C6H5NH2 + HNO2 → C6H5N2+Cl- + H2O
  - C6H5N2+Cl- + H2O → Phenol + N2 + HCl

- **From coal tar:** Coal tar is a dark brown liquid obtained from the distillation of coal. It contains various aromatic compounds, including phenol. When coal tar is fractionally distilled, phenol is obtained from the middle oil fraction at a temperature range of 443-503 K. This is one of the industrial methods of preparing phenol.



### Formaldehyde

- Formaldehyde is a naturally occurring organic compound with the formula CH2O and structure H−CHO.
- Formaldehyde is a colorless, highly toxic, and flammable gas at room temperature.
- Formaldehyde is an intermediate in the oxidation (or combustion) of methane, as well as of other carbon compounds, e.g. in forest fires, automobile exhaust, and tobacco smoke.
- Formaldehyde is used in the production of fertilizer, paper, plywood, and some resins. It is also used as a food preservative and in household products, such as antiseptics, medicines, and cosmetics.
- Formaldehyde is a carcinogen, meaning it can cause cancer in humans and animals . Exposure to formaldehyde can also cause irritation of the eyes, nose, throat, and skin, as well as respiratory problems, allergic reactions, and neurological effects .

### Urea formaldehyde resin

- Urea formaldehyde resin is a synthetic polymer made by reacting urea and formaldehyde in an aqueous solution.
- Urea formaldehyde resin is a thermosetting resin, meaning it can be cured by heat and pressure to form a hard and durable material.
- Urea formaldehyde resin is widely used as an adhesive for wood products, such as particleboard, plywood, and medium-density fiberboard (MDF). It is also used as a coating for paper, textiles, and electrical appliances.
- Urea formaldehyde resin has some advantages over other resins, such as low cost, high strength, water resistance, and fire resistance.
- Urea formaldehyde resin also has some disadvantages, such as emitting formaldehyde gas during curing and aging, which can cause health and environmental problems. Urea formaldehyde resin can also degrade under high humidity and temperature, leading to reduced performance and durability.

### Adipic acid

- Adipic acid is an organic compound with the formula C6H10O4 and structure HOOC−(CH2)4−COOH.
- Adipic acid is a white, crystalline solid at room temperature, with a melting point of 152 °C and a boiling point of 337 °C.
- Adipic acid is mainly produced by oxidation of cyclohexane or cyclohexanol with nitric acid, followed by purification and crystallization.
- Adipic acid is mainly used as a precursor for the synthesis of nylon 6,6, a widely used synthetic fiber and plastic. Adipic acid is also used as a food additive, a flavoring agent, a plasticizer, a lubricant, and a component of some pharmaceuticals.
- Adipic acid is generally considered to be a safe and biodegradable substance, with low toxicity and environmental impact. However, adipic acid can cause skin and eye irritation, and ingestion can cause nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea. Adipic acid can also contribute to acid rain and greenhouse gas emissions, due to the use of nitric acid and the release of nitrous oxide during its production.

### Paracetamol

- Paracetamol is an organic compound with the formula C8H9NO2 and structure HOC6H4NHCOCH3.
- Paracetamol is a white, crystalline powder at room temperature, with a melting point of 169 °C and a boiling point of 420 °C.
- Paracetamol is mainly produced by acetylation of 4-aminophenol with acetic anhydride, followed by purification and crystallization.
- Paracetamol is mainly used as a pain reliever and a fever reducer, for conditions such as headache, toothache, arthritis, and cold. Paracetamol is also used as an ingredient in some cough and cold medicines, and some combination products with other drugs.
- Paracetamol is generally considered to be a safe and effective drug, with few side effects and interactions. However, paracetamol can cause liver damage and even death, if taken in large doses or with alcohol. Paracetamol can also cause allergic reactions, such as skin rash, swelling, and difficulty breathing, in some people



### K3

- K3 is a type of visa that allows a foreign spouse of a U.S. citizen to enter and reside in the United States while waiting for the approval of their immigrant visa petition.
- K3 visa holders are eligible to apply for a work permit and travel authorization while in the United States.
- K3 visa is valid for two years and can be extended if the immigrant visa process is not completed within that time.
- K3 visa is not a direct path to permanent residency or citizenship. K3 visa holders must still go through the adjustment of status process or consular processing to obtain a green card.
- K3 visa is intended to reduce the separation time between the U.S. citizen and their foreign spouse, but it is not always faster or easier than the immigrant visa process. K3 visa applicants must meet the same eligibility and documentary requirements as immigrant visa applicants, and they must also undergo a medical examination and a security check.



### CO-5 Estimate the rate constant of reaction. K3

- The rate constant of a reaction, K3, is a measure of how fast the reaction proceeds at a given temperature and concentration of reactants.
- The rate constant can be determined experimentally by measuring the rate of the reaction under different conditions and plotting the data on a graph.
- The rate law of a reaction relates the rate of the reaction to the concentration of the reactants and the rate constant. The rate law can be written as:

`rate = K3[A]^m[B]^n`

where A and B are the reactants, m and n are the orders of the reaction with respect to A and B, and K3 is the rate constant.

- The orders of the reaction can be determined by varying the concentration of one reactant while keeping the other constant and observing the effect on the rate. The order of the reaction with respect to a reactant is equal to the exponent of its concentration in the rate law.
- The rate constant can be calculated by substituting the values of the rate, the concentrations, and the orders of the reaction into the rate law and solving for K3.
- Alternatively, the rate constant can be estimated by using the Arrhenius equation, which relates the rate constant to the activation energy, the temperature, and the frequency factor of the reaction. The Arrhenius equation can be written as:

`K3 = A*e^(-Ea/RT)`

where A is the frequency factor, Ea is the activation energy, R is the gas constant, and T is the temperature in kelvin.

- The frequency factor and the activation energy can be determined by measuring the rate constant at different temperatures and plotting the natural logarithm of the rate constant versus the inverse of the temperature on a graph. The slope of the graph is equal to -Ea/R and the intercept is equal to ln(A).
- The rate constant of a reaction can vary depending on the temperature, the concentration of the reactants, the presence of catalysts, and other factors that affect the rate of the reaction. Therefore, the rate constant should be reported with the units, the temperature, and the conditions under which it was measured or estimated.
